**Summary.** This paper introduces a machine-learning technique to improve Quantum Error Correction by filtering out noisy runs based solely on syndrome measurements. By training a classifier to distinguish between low- and high-noise syndromes, the method provides a scalable, hardware-compatible post-selection score. This approach successfully enhances fidelity in both simulated codes and real neutral-atom quantum computing experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a practical, data-driven tool for improving quantum hardware performance that bypasses complex theoretical assumptions about decoding thresholds, making it highly relevant to experimental quantum information scientists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to enhance Quantum Error Correction (QEC) reliability by post-selecting runs likely to fail, circumventing the need for computationally expensive decoder-level information.

**Main result.** A machine-learning approach using syndrome data alone can significantly reduce conditional logical error rates and improve output fidelity in both simulated codes and real experimental platforms.

**Method.** The authors train a supervised binary classifier on syndrome measurements, distinguishing between syndromes generated under low-noise versus high-noise conditions. This classifier's output serves as a decoder-agnostic 'abort score' for run rejection.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general QEC codes (e.g., Surface Code, Gross bivariate-bicycle code) and is validated using experimental data from magic-state distillation on a neutral-atom processor.

**Key observables.** Conditional logical error rate ($	ilde{p}_L$), post-selection acceptance rate ($R$), and output fidelity ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rates ($p$), code distance ($d$), and the machine-learning cutoff threshold ($C_0$ or $p_{ML}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is explicitly designed to be decoder-agnostic, relying only on syndrome distributions rather than requiring knowledge of logical operators or specific decoding algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the training process using low/high-noise ensembles and show performance comparisons (e.g., fidelity vs. acceptance rate) where ML+LG outperforms LG alone in experimental settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the supervised classification methodology trained on synthetic noise ensembles, validates the approach across three distinct regimes (circuit simulation, code-capacity simulation, and real experiment), and concludes by demonstrating its scalability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction can be enhanced by post-selecting out runs that are likely to produce a logical failure, but the most accurate measures for that require costly decoder-level information. We introduce a practical, decoder-agnostic post-selection method that learns directly from syndrome data. The method trains a supervised classifier to distinguish between syndromes from low- and high-noise regimes, and then uses the classifier's output as an abort score for new runs, without requiring logical-error labels, correction operators, or code-specific likelihood calculations. We validate the approach in three complementary settings: circuit-level simulations of the Gross bivariate-bicycle code, code-capacity simulations of the surface code, and experimental logical magic-state distillation data from the QuEra neutral-atom processor. In the Gross and surface codes, learned syndrome post-selection reduces the conditional logical error rate at a fixed acceptance rate, with performance comparable to syndrome-weight filtering. For the surface code, the learned classifier reveals a post-selection transition distinct from the conventional decoding threshold. In the experimental data, the machine-learning score outperforms syndrome-weight post-selection and, when combined with logical-gap filtering, improves the output fidelity beyond using the logical gap alone. These results show that syndrome-only learning provides a scalable and hardware-compatible route to improving the reliability of quantum error correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper empirically tests how well the geometric structure encoded by a four-qubit ZZ quantum kernel survives execution on real quantum hardware using various error mitigation techniques. The authors find that while certain methods improve geometric preservation, this fidelity does not guarantee optimal performance relative to external task labels. Ultimately, it serves as a cautionary diagnostic study highlighting the distinct nature of implementation fidelity versus task relevance in near-term quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, empirical test case for quantum machine learning pipelines, demonstrating the critical need to decouple hardware implementation fidelity from potential downstream predictive utility in noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine if the intended geometry of a quantum kernel survives execution when implemented on real, noisy quantum hardware.

**Main result.** While gate twirling provided the most faithful reconstruction of the designed geometry, this best-performing configuration did not yield the highest alignment with external task labels, suggesting fidelity and task relevance are distinct properties.

**Method.** The authors compared three error mitigation strategies (baseline, dynamical decoupling, gate twirling) by measuring kernel geometry preservation using multiple diagnostics on a fixed dataset.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a fixed four-qubit ZZ feature map kernel applied to $N=24$ indoor air-quality data points, executed on IBM Quantum hardware (ibm_fez).

**Key observables.** Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA), centered kernel–target alignment (KTA), Spearman rank correlation, and effective rank.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to a fixed subset of $N=24$ data points using 1024 shots per circuit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are purely descriptive diagnostics for single jobs on one backend; they do not constitute causal estimates of mitigation efficacy or quantum advantage.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Notes refer to supplementary figures/tables detailing diagnostic metrics).

**Paper structure.** The paper diagnoses geometry survival by comparing the performance of three error mitigation techniques across various quantitative diagnostics, concluding that hardware distortion dominates and separating geometric fidelity from task-specific label alignment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum-kernel methods encode a dataset's geometry in a Gram matrix, so learning claims on hardware kernels assume the intended geometry survives execution. We measure that survival for one frozen four-qubit ZZ feature-map kernel on $N=24$ real indoor air-quality windows, reconstructed on ibm_fez (1024 shots per circuit) under baseline, dynamical decoupling alone, and gate twirling alone, each a single non-interleaved job. Every configuration returned a complete, finite, positive-semidefinite Gram matrix and preserved the centered statevector geometry to a substantial but incomplete descriptive degree (full-matrix centered kernel alignment, CKA, 0.933-0.989). Gate twirling was most faithful on every reported geometry axis, with the only jackknife-resolved improvement over baseline (persisted Spearman, mean absolute error, and full-matrix CKA diagnostics); dynamical decoupling alone was not separated from baseline at the frozen-window scale. Residual hardware distortion, not finite sampling, dominates the discrepancy. Yet fidelity and label alignment were reversed: the most faithful configuration had the lowest centered kernel-target alignment, which sits at or below label-permutation references for statevector and hardware alike. We read the small hardware uplift as a normalization property of the non-affine distortion, not captured signal. These are descriptive results for single jobs on one backend, not causal mitigation-efficacy estimates; no quantum-advantage, hardware-classifier-superiority, or forecasting claim is made. Implementation fidelity and task relevance are distinct axes; hardware quantum machine-learning studies should report both.

</details>

**Authors:** Yanjun Chu, Yuhang Xie, Chenyang Cui, Shao-Ming Fei  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20311v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

The blue points in the four subplots represent the trace distance be- tween the state ρAB and the 5 × 104,105,5 × 105 and 106 randomly generated block textureless states, respectively.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum-State Block Texture (QSBT) as an advanced resource measure for bipartite quantum states. It pioneers several distinct mathematical frameworks—including geometric and trace distance approaches—to quantify this 'texture.' The results establish relationships between these measures, providing a deeper understanding of how different metrics characterize the non-local structure of quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework to quantify subtle structural features ('texture') in quantum states beyond standard measures like entanglement or coherence, which is crucial for designing protocols that exploit specific state properties.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes Quantum-State Texture (QST) to Quantum-State Block Texture (QSBT) to provide operational measures for quantifying the quantum advantage embedded in bipartite quantum states.

**Main result.** Multiple proposed QSBT measures, including geometric ($M_g$), trace distance ($M_{tr}$), fidelity ($M_f$), and relative entropy ($M_r$), are shown to be distinct but related, with $M_g$ serving as an upper bound for $M_{tr}$.

**Method.** The authors develop structural frameworks by defining necessary conditions (T1-T4) for QSBT measures and deriving explicit formulas for various metrics based on minimizing distances/entropies relative to block textureless states.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on bipartite quantum states $ho_{AB}$ in a Hilbert space $H_A \otimes H_B$, comparing them against the set of 'block textureless' states, $\mathcal{T}_B$.

**Key observables.** Quantum-State Block Texture (QSBT) measures: $M_g(ho_{AB})$, $M_{tr}(ho_{AB})$, $M_f(ho_{AB})$, and $M_r(ho_{AB})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the bipartite system ($A \otimes B$) and the specific family of states used for numerical sampling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory builds upon existing resource theories (like coherence) and assumes the validity of structural conditions (T1-T4) for quantifying QSBT.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare different measures, showing that $M_{tr}$ distinguishes state families differently from block state rugosity, and verifying relationships like $M_g \ge [M_{tr}]^2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by generalizing QST to QSBT, proposing multiple quantitative measures ($M_g, M_{tr}, M_f, M_r$), establishing theoretical properties (e.g., Theorem III.1 for $Q_{AB}$), and finally comparing the distinct characteristics of these derived metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum-state texture (QST) is an emerging quantum resource that has garnered increasing attention amid advances in quantum theory. In this work, we generalize the QST to quantum-state block texture (QSBT). This generalization provides profound operational interpretations for quantifying the advantages of quantum states in quantum information processing. We pioneer an alternative framework for characterizing and quantifying quantum-state block texture, and propose three types of block texture measures. By comparing these QSBT measures, we investigate their distinctions and interrelationships. We demonstrate that the geometric measure serves as an upper bound for the trace distance-based measure. For a specific family of quantum states, we evaluate the values of two trace distance-based measures. Then we sample four sets of data from this family of states, with each set comprising $5\times 10^4, 10^5, 5\times 10^5$ and $10^6 $ samples, respectively, and present the corresponding distributions. Our results reveal that the QSBT measures constructed via different approaches show distinct characteristics, indicating their potential roles in quantifying the block texture of quantum states.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details a method to create nonreciprocal phonon blockade in an optomechanical system by exploiting the Sagnac-Fizeau effect in two coupled spinning resonators. This asymmetry causes one direction of light pumping to exhibit conventional phonon blockade, while the opposite direction facilitates phonon-induced tunneling. The work provides a pathway for directional quantum control devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it combines several advanced topics: cavity QED (optomechanics), nonlinear quantum optics (blockade), and directional control (nonreciprocity) using a specific physical effect (Sagnac-Fizeau).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a scheme to achieve nonreciprocal phonon blockade (PB) using a quadratic optomechanical (QOM) system.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that the Sagnac-Fizeau effect creates direction-dependent mechanical frequency shifts, leading to conventional PB in one pump direction and facilitating two-phonon resonance/tunneling (PIT) in the opposite direction.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving an effective Hamiltonian for quantum fluctuations and analyzing the system's dynamics using the Lindblad master equation framework.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a QOM system comprising two spinning Whispering Gallery Mode (WGM) resonators coupled to a nanomechanical oscillator. The nonreciprocity stems from the Sagnac-Fizeau effect causing asymmetric detunings for counter-propagating pump fields.

**Key observables.** Phonon second-order correlation function ($g^{(2)}_b(0)$), which quantifies blockade (antibunching) or bunching, and direction-dependent effective mechanical frequencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis relies on the resolved supermode regime ($J \gg \omega_m, \kappa$) and is sensitive to parameters governing the Sagnac-Fizeau shift ($\Delta F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions include operating in the weak optomechanical coupling limit and using an analytical wave-function approximation consistent with master equation simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the setup (two spinning WGM resonators coupled to a mechanical oscillator) and shows energy level diagrams detailing PB under single-phonon resonance and PIT under two-phonon resonance.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical system and the mechanism of nonreciprocity via the Sagnac-Fizeau effect. It then analyzes the resulting quantum statistics, quantifying the blockade contrast ratio, and finally extends the analysis to include thermal phonons to study extended nonreciprocal effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a scheme for achieving nonreciprocal phonon blockade in a quadratic optomechanical (QOM) system consisting of two spinning resonators near-field coupled to a nanomechanical oscillator. Due to the Sagnac-Fizeau effect, pump fields propagating in opposite directions experience distinct effective detunings,thereby leading to asymmetric intracavity intensities. Through the optical spring effect, this intensity imbalance gives rise to direction-dependent shifts in the effective mechanical frequency, providing the core mechanism for nonreciprocal phonon blockade. By judiciously setting parameters, single-phonon resonant excitation leads to conventional phonon blockade for one pump direction, whereas two-phonon resonance facilitates phononinduced tunneling (PIT) for the other. The pronounced nonreciprocity is quantified by a contrast ratio in the phonon second-order correlation function exceeding 55 dB. To elucidate the nonreciprocal statistics, the phonon blockade is further analyzed in terms of interference between the coherent component and squeezed fluctuations. Incorporating thermal phonons, we reveal an extended nonreciprocal thermal effect, where increasing thermal noise degrades antibunching toward Poissonian statistics in one direction, yet reverses the statistics from bunching to antibunching in the opposite direction. Our work provides a pathway toward nonreciprocal phonon devices and directional phonon switches, with potential applications in chiral networks and phononic information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a rigorous framework using SU(2) mapping to analyze matter-wave Bell interferometers, enabling the separation of geometric dephasing from true environmental decoherence. By analyzing measurements at zero path difference, the authors extract a source-independent measure of entanglement loss. This provides a concrete diagnostic tool for testing fundamental physics, such as mass-dependent decoherence mechanisms in quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalizable theoretical tool for interpreting high-precision quantum measurements in cold atom systems. By rigorously separating noise sources, it elevates the field's ability to distinguish fundamental physics signals from technical artifacts, which is crucial for next-generation quantum sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a robust theoretical framework to disentangle geometric dephasing, environmental decoherence, and technical measurement limitations when interpreting Bell correlations in matter-wave interferometers.

**Main result.** The authors present a method using the zero path difference (ZPD) condition that yields a source-distribution-independent extraction of the true Bell correlation amplitude reduction. They show this framework can set concrete null benchmarks for detecting mass-dependent decoherence in dual-species systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Schwinger SU(2) mapping to model momentum-mode occupation states as pseudospin operators, allowing systematic separation of unitary (geometric) and dissipative (decoherence) noise channels.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a matter-wave Bell interferometer, specifically utilizing Rarity-Tapster geometries with momentum-entangled atom pairs. The theory models the measurement process using spin-$rac{1}{2}$ operators on a Bloch sphere.

**Key observables.** Bell correlation amplitude ($\langle A angle$), which quantifies entanglement; the coincidence fringe measured at zero path difference (ZPD).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero interferometer path difference ($\delta x = 0$); low-mode-occupancy limit ($ar{n} \ll 1$); mass difference between species in dual-species tests.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a low-mode-occupancy regime and relies on the symmetry properties of the interferometer geometry (e.g., RT symmetry) to cancel certain systematic errors like linear magnetic gradients.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup of the Rarity-Tapster interferometer, the mapping from momentum modes to pseudospin states on a Bloch sphere, and how path asymmetry ($\delta x$) introduces measurable geometric phase shifts.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the measurement formalism using SU(2) mapping, then deriving the dependence of the correlation function on path differences ($\delta x$), identifying ZPD as the key diagnostic point to eliminate geometric dephasing, and finally extending this framework to test for differential decoherence between atomic species.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Matter-wave Bell interferometers provide a sensitive probe of mass-dependent decoherence in entangled quantum systems. The degree of entanglement is obtained from the Bell-correlation amplitude of this interferometer. For observing potential mass-dependent decoherence, a reliable interpretation of any observed reduction in the Bell correlation amplitude is required, which depends on three factors: geometric dephasing, environmental decoherence, and technical dilution from source and detection statistics. In this work, we present a framework based on the Schwinger SU(2) mapping to separate these contributions into local unitaries or dissipative channels. We show that by evaluating the Bell correlation at zero interferometer path difference, it is possible to extract a source-distribution-independent Bell correlation amplitude reduction. When this framework is extended to involve atoms of different mass, we show that the known differential decoherence channels are negligible at current sensitivity. This yields a concrete bound at which a dual-species Bell interferometer would begin to signal differential decoherence beyond the known systematics, opening the way for such systems to probe new physics, such as mass-dependent decoherence mechanisms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes an effective model demonstrating how structured light can mediate the creation of hybrid entanglement between emitted photon polarization and the orbital angular momentum stored in an electron within a semiconductor quantum disk. By analyzing the system dynamics via master equations, it shows that high fidelity is achievable provided electronic orbital relaxation is slow compared to the emission timescale.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum information processing by proposing a solid-state platform for generating photonic qubits entangled with internal electronic degrees of freedom (OAM), which is highly relevant to quantum networking and memory interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is generating hybrid entanglement between photon polarization and electronic orbital angular momentum (OAM) using structured light in a semiconductor quantum disk.

**Main result.** The proposed effective model demonstrates that high-fidelity entanglement can be achieved when the orbital relaxation time significantly exceeds the characteristic emission and detection timescales.

**Method.** The analysis employs an open-system master equation approach to track the system's evolution, calculating heralded entanglement fidelity conditioned on single-photon detection.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a semiconductor quantum disk utilizing two excitation channels: one for radiative recombination (zero OAM) and another storing the electronic orbital qubit (finite OAM). The dynamics are modeled using an effective Hamiltonian and Lindblad dissipators.

**Key observables.** Heralded entanglement fidelity ($F(t)$), single-photon probability ($P_{1ph}(t)$), and the conditional electronic state $	ilde{ho}_{B\gamma}(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Orbital relaxation rate ($\Gamma_{orb}$), radiative decay rate ($\Gamma_{rad}$), and the effective two-photon Rabi coupling ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model is a proof-of-principle effective description, neglecting microscopic details like biexciton binding or external losses. High fidelity requires $	au_{orb} > 	au_{	ext{emission/detection}}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of single-photon probability and the heralded entanglement fidelity as a function of pulse width and orbital relaxation rate, respectively.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical mapping using structured light to create two excitation channels. It then models the subsequent radiative recombination dynamics using an open-system master equation approach to calculate the conditional state and entanglement fidelity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a minimal quantum-optical scheme for generating hybrid entanglement between photon polarization and electronic orbital angular momentum in a semiconductor quantum disk. A spin--orbit structured two-photon state excites two channels in the same disk: a radiatively recombining zero-orbital-angular-momentum channel and a finite-orbital-angular-momentum channel that stores the electronic orbital qubit. An effective coherent mapping prepares a selected two-excitation state, followed by emission of a photon whose polarization is entangled with the residual electronic orbital state. A master-equation analysis shows that the heralded state conditioned on single-photon occupation of the selected output mode approaches the target entangled state in the ideal coherent limit. We also discuss orbital relaxation and perturbative validity conditions for branch-dependent Coulomb shifts, orbital-angular-momentum mixing, and finite-orbital-angular-momentum radiative leakage. This proof-of-principle effective model suggests a route toward structured-light-mediated photon--electron hybrid entanglement in semiconductor nanostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides theoretical predictions for detecting loop current orders—spontaneous orbital currents—in quantum materials like cuprates and $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$. By simulating STM/STS measurements on tight-binding models, the authors show that such order manifests as measurable spectral gaps in the DOS and characteristic patterns in QPI maps. These predictions guide future experimental efforts to confirm this exotic form of electronic ordering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter physics, the detailed mapping of symmetry breaking (time-reversal symmetry) to measurable spectroscopic fingerprints is highly relevant to understanding emergent order parameters in strongly correlated systems, which shares conceptual links with many-body dynamics studied in AMO contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop specific theoretical predictions for how loop current orders, which break time-reversal symmetry, can be detected using low temperature Scanning Tunneling Microscopy and Spectroscopy (STM/STS) in quantum materials.

**Main result.** Loop currents are predicted to cause a splitting of Van Hove singularities (VHS) in the local density of states (DOS), leading to partial energy gaps ($\sim 10-20 	ext{ meV}$), and they predict the emergence of measurable spin polarization detectable by spin-polarized STM.

**Method.** The authors use continuum Green's function methods applied to tight-binding models, simulating spectroscopic maps (LDOS) and Quasi-Particle Interference (QPI) patterns for both cuprates and $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$.

**Model / system.** The study applies theoretical modeling to high-temperature cuprate superconductors and the surface layer of $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$, incorporating loop current terms ($\hat{H}_{	ext{LC}}$) into tight-binding Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Spectroscopic maps (DOS), QPI patterns with characteristic scattering vectors, and finite spin polarization detectable by spin-polarized STM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Loop current strength ($m$), energy gap size ($\Delta E \sim 10-20 	ext{ meV}$), and the presence/absence of Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations simplify by assuming staggered loop currents across the unit cell, and they neglect potential self-energy effects accompanying the order formation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate DOS splitting and QPI changes in both cuprate and $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ models as a function of loop current strength ($m$), alongside predicted spin polarization maps for SP-STM detection.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical context (loop currents in superconductors), details the theoretical framework using tight-binding models, and then presents specific predictions regarding observable signatures—namely DOS splitting, QPI changes, and spin polarization—for both cuprates and ruthenates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The emergence of loop current phases, where spontaneous loops of orbital currents give rise to a weak local magnetic moments, has been proposed to exist in a number of quantum materials based on measurements that pick up weak signatures of time reversal symmetry breaking or small magnetic moment order. The most prominent example is as an explanation of the pseudogap phase on the underdoped side of the phase diagram of the high-temperature cuprate superconductors, but more recently, it has been proposed to occur in Kagome materials and at the surface layer of Sr$_2$RuO$_4$. Experimental results have, however, been inconclusive so far, some detecting signatures that can be understood as emerging due to loop current phases, whilst others have not detected any significant proof. One of the techniques that should be able to pick up local signatures of loop current orders is low temperature scanning tunneling microscopy and spectroscopy (STM/STS), however firm predictions of how to detect them are missing. Here, we provide specific predictions for how loop current orders in a square lattice can be seen in spectroscopic maps, using models of the cuprate high-temperature superconductors and of the surface layer of Sr$_2$RuO$_4$. We find that, besides lifting degeneracies at the specific ordering vector of the loop current order, a finite spin polarisation emerges when spin-orbit coupling is present, signatures of which can be detected in spin-polarised STM.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the dynamics of vortex beams propagating through a three-level atomic medium using forward and backward three-wave mixing. By comparing both processes, the authors demonstrate that the backward TWM scheme is superior for generating high-gain, high-fidelity structured light. The findings suggest practical applications in quantum communication and computation utilizing OAM.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum optics phenomena—specifically structured light propagation and nonlinear interactions in atomic media—which are foundational to developing integrated photonic components for quantum communication.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how high-gain vortex beams propagate through symmetry-broken media using both forward and backward three-wave mixing (TWM) processes.

**Main result.** Backward TWM was found to yield superior performance compared to the forward process, exhibiting higher signal gain, improved fidelity, and more stable transmission characteristics.

**Method.** The authors employed a perturbative approximation and steady-state assumption to solve the coupled Maxwell-Bloch equations derived from the system Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The model is a three-level ladder-type atomic system exhibiting broken inversion symmetry, driven by a strong control field ($\omega_c$) interacting with weak probe ($\omega_p$) and signal ($\omega_s$) fields.

**Key observables.** Vortex light properties, including topological charge (OAM), gain, fidelity, transmission characteristics, and spatial profile modulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the Autler-Townes Splitting (ATS) regime; detuning ($\Delta_p$) has a negligible influence on gain, while optical depth only affects the rate of reaching maximum gain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating the control field as strong and continuous ($|\Omega_c| \gg |\Omega_p|, |\Omega_s|$), using the perturbative approximation, and assuming steady-state conditions ($\partial/\partial t = 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the symmetry-broken three-level ladder system setup. Figure 2 likely shows relative intensities of probe and signal fields during forward propagation under varying decay rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using a Hamiltonian, derives coupled equations of motion via the Liouville equation, applies approximations to simplify these equations, and then solves the resulting steady-state Maxwell-Bloch equations for both Forward TWM (FTWM) and Backward TWM (BTWM).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In recent years, vortex light, a distinctive form of structured light, has attracted considerable attention owing to its unique properties and the rich physical phenomena arising from its interaction with matter. In this paper, we investigate the propagation dynamics of vortex beams in a symmetry-broken three-level system based on forward and backward three-wave mixing (TWM) processes. We find that both processes enable the transfer of high-gain vortex light, with the associated topological charges obeying identical algebraic relations. The forward process exhibits periodic oscillatory transmission and modulates the transverse spatial profile of the generated signal vortex field, whereas the backward process features stable transmission and produces a signal field with higher gain and improved fidelity. Under the Autler-Townes splitting (ATS) regime, the probe field detuning in both schemes has a negligible influence on the gain. Optical depth influences only the rate at which the gain approaches its maximum, while the peak value remains unchanged. These results constitute a meaningful extension of the work reported in reference [40] and may provide a feasible approach for quantum communication, quantum computation, and the generation of high-gain, high-fidelity vortex light.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details a method to create mechanical Schrödinger cat states in an optically levitated nanoparticle by coupling it to the momentum kicks from single electrons. By controlling the phase of these electron time-bin states, high-visibility interference fringes are generated, allowing for tests of quantum mechanics on unprecedentedly large objects while mitigating common decoherence sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental questions about quantum mechanics at the mesoscopic scale by providing a robust experimental pathway that leverages single-particle interactions to manipulate macroscopic quantum states, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors propose a novel protocol to prepare and observe mesoscopic quantum superpositions of mechanical motion (Schrödinger kittens) in an optically levitated nanoparticle.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that coherence can be maintained over single oscillation periods by coupling the particle's state to controllable, phase-dependent momentum kicks imparted by single electrons. This method bypasses limitations of previous techniques for testing quantum mechanics at macroscopic scales.

**Method.** The protocol involves using time-bin states of single electrons to induce a superposition of momentum kicks ($\pm \delta p$) onto the levitated nanoparticle, followed by interferometric measurement of the particle's position probability distribution.

**Model / system.** The system is an amorphous $	ext{SiO}_2$ bead trapped in a harmonic potential. The quantum dynamics are analyzed using master equations and Wigner/characteristic functions to track decoherence from sources like gas collisions and trap fluctuations.

**Key observables.** Interference fringes in the particle's Center-of-Mass (CoM) position probability $P_{\Delta x}(\phi, x)$, non-classicality confirmed by Wigner negativity, and interference visibility ($V$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The momentum kick $\delta p$ is controlled via external electric fields; decoherence rates ($\Gamma$) are compared against the trap frequency ($\omega$), with timescales set relative to the oscillation period ($\pi/\omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that the state preparation time ($\delta t$) is short enough such that harmonic motion and decoherence can be treated as approximately frozen during the kick application.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the interference pattern $P_{\Delta x}(\phi, x)$ varying sinusoidally with electron phase $\phi$, show Wigner function negativity for non-classical states, and plot visibility dependence on initial occupation number and decoherence rate.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from proposing the protocol using time-bin electrons to induce momentum superpositions. It mathematically models the evolution of the quantum state (using master equations/Wigner functions) while analyzing various sources of decoherence, culminating in predictions for observable interference fringes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a protocol for preparing mechanical Schrödinger kittens -- mesoscopic quantum superpositions of coherent motional states of an optically levitated nanoparticle -- by adhering single electron time-bin states to its surface. Over short protocol timescales, coherence of the mesoscopic superposition survives the dominant decoherence mechanisms afflicting levitated systems, and can be observed by varying the phase of the time-bin electrons. Interference fringes can be detected with real-time, near-Heisenberg limited interferometry of photons scattered from the particle. This approach eliminates the need for coherent state expansion, dark potentials, and particle release-and-recapture mechanisms, providing a new route to test quantum mechanics in unprecedented scales.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quantitative adiabatic theorem for non-Hermitian quantum dynamics by transforming the problem into an equivalent Hermitian system using a Dyson map. It derives an explicit error bound that accounts for both the standard adiabatic approximation error and the additional error introduced by the transformation itself. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for analyzing time evolution in open or dissipative quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO physics and open quantum systems because non-Hermiticity naturally arises when coupling quantum systems to an environment or when considering gain/loss processes. Providing a rigorous adiabatic framework for these dynamics is crucial for modeling physical reality beyond simple unitary evolution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a rigorous, quantitative adiabatic estimate for quantum systems whose dynamics are governed by non-Hermitian Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The authors establish an explicit error bound for the adiabatic approximation in non-Hermitian settings by transforming the system into an equivalent Hermitian representation and quantifying the transformation error.

**Method.** The method involves constructing a dynamically compatible Dyson map using a metric operator to transform the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_T$) into a Hermitian counterpart ($ar{H}_T$). The standard adiabatic theorem is then applied in this transformed space, with errors quantified via contour-resolvent estimates.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to finite-dimensional quantum systems evolving under time-dependent, non-Hermitian Schrödinger dynamics. A two-level model is used to illustrate the full scope of the theorem and its error components.

**Key observables.** The final estimate controls the evolution operator error $|e^{U_{A, \pm}(s)} - \hat{U}_T(s)|$, and the analysis tracks spectral projections and gap sizes in the transformed Hermitian system.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time parameter $s$ (ranging from 0 to 1), the spectral gap of the effective Hermitian Hamiltonian $ar{H}_T(s)$, and the adiabatic constant $T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include that the original non-Hermitian Hamiltonian must be diagonalizable with a real spectrum, possess non-crossing eigenvalues, and satisfy a contour-resolvent condition for the transformed system.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not describe specific figures, but mention the final estimate containing two explicit error contributions: the pulled-back Hermitian adiabatic error and the projection-comparison error, illustrated by the two-level model.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the need for an adiabatic theorem extension to non-Hermitian dynamics. It then introduces the Dyson map construction to achieve a Hermitian representation ($ar{H}_T$). Finally, it applies standard adiabatic techniques in this transformed space and pulls the resulting estimate back to yield the final bound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a quantitative adiabatic estimate for a class of finite-dimensional non-Hermitian Schrödinger dynamics. The original non-Hermitian Hamiltonian is assumed to be diagonalizable with real spectrum and non-crossing eigenvalues. We then construct a dynamically compatible time-dependent metric operator, and its positive square root defines a Dyson map. The associated Dyson-transformed Hamiltonian is Hermitian.   When this Dyson-transformed Hamiltonian satisfies the Hermitian adiabatic assumption, the standard resolvent-projection method gives an explicit adiabatic estimate in the Hermitian representation. Pulling this estimate back through the Dyson map gives an approximation in the original non-Hermitian representation. The resulting Dyson-pulled-back projections are then compared with the spectral projections of the original non-Hermitian Hamiltonian by using a contour-resolvent estimate. The final bound contains two contributions: the pulled-back Hermitian adiabatic error and the projection-comparison error. A two-level non-Hermitian model is presented to illustrate the hypotheses of the theorem and the two contributions appearing in the final estimate.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that exploiting quantum resonance can dramatically boost the performance of quantum batteries, allowing them to charge power linearly over time while maintaining near-perfect energy efficiency. This enhancement is robust and persists even when strong entanglement builds up within the coupled system models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects fundamental concepts from quantum thermodynamics (energy storage/extraction) with phenomena arising from periodically driven systems (quantum resonance), providing a concrete resource for energy management in quantum technologies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether quantum resonance can enhance the performance of quantum batteries, specifically aiming for stable charging power increase with time and near-unity efficiency despite strong entanglement generation.

**Main result.** Quantum resonance leads to a linear increase in charging power and maintains near-unity energy extraction efficiency ($\eta \approx 1$) even when significant entanglement is generated. This enhancement mechanism is shown to be universal across different models.

**Method.** The authors use analytical derivations involving Bessel functions and numerical simulations to calculate stored energy, extractable work (ergotropy), and efficiency for coupled quantum rotor and kicked top systems.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum batteries using free rotors or interacting kicked tops. The dynamics are governed by time-periodic Hamiltonians incorporating both intrinsic quadratic terms ($H_0$) and nonlinear interaction/kicking potentials ($V(x_1, x_2)$).

**Key observables.** Stored Energy ($E(	au)$), Extractable Work (Ergotropy $\xi(	au)$), Efficiency ($\eta(	au) = \xi/	ext{stored energy}$), and Linear Entropy ($S_L(	au)$ for entanglement).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quantum Resonance condition (rational ratio of intrinsic to driving frequency); specific resonance conditions like $\hbar s T = 4\pi$ or $K_2 \gg K$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations for large time ($	au$) and specific parameter limits, such as the regime where one interaction strength dominates ($K_2 \gg K$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate linear growth of charging power at resonance (Fig. 1a), efficiency remaining near unity under resonance (Fig. 1b), and confirmation of this effect in higher-order resonances for both rotor and kicked top models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model using free rotors, deriving analytical results showing linear power growth at resonance. It then generalizes this to an interacting kicked top model, confirming the universality of the enhancement mechanism across different physical realizations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum resonance arising whenever the ratio of the intrinsic system frequency to the driving frequency becomes a rational number has been demonstrated to generate super-linear entanglement, enhance transport, quantum metrology performance and communication. Here, we demonstrate that quantum resonance can also serve as a powerful resource for quantum batteries. We model the batteries as free rotors charged via a kicked protocol. When the individual batteries are at resonance, we show both analytically and numerically that charging power increases linearly with time while efficiency (defined as the fraction of stored energy that can be extracted) remains near unity despite strong entanglement generation. Furthermore, we demonstrate that this enhanced performance persists at higher-order resonances. Demonstrating the universality of this mechanism, we show that similar enhancements arise in the interacting kicked top model, and briefly note the feasibility of its experimental realization. In a broader context, resonant charging holds significant implications for energy storage, quantum computational resources, and quantum thermodynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper models a 1D lattice system exhibiting an ordered phase that typically gets stuck in an absorbing state. By introducing biased motility through particle hopping, the authors show this activity destabilizes the order, driving a continuous transition to a disordered state. The findings are quantitatively confirmed using finite-size scaling analysis of critical exponents.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative insights into how energy injection or active transport (motility) can fundamentally change the steady-state behavior of interacting classical systems, moving them away from simple absorbing traps towards dynamic disorder.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how introducing activity, specifically biased motility, affects an ordered phase that otherwise settles into an absorbing state in a many-body system.

**Main result.** Motility destabilizes the absorbing ferromagnetic state, causing a continuous 'deflocking' transition to a disordered state. The critical exponents satisfy hyperscaling relations for one dimension.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on finite-size scaling techniques applied to physical quantities like the Binder cumulant and global magnetization to characterize the phase transition.

**Model / system.** A one-dimensional lattice model using Ising spins ($\sigma_i = \pm 1$) with local interactions. The dynamics are governed by a combination of spin-flip rules (non-motile limit) and an asynchronous hopping/flipping process that introduces motility.

**Key observables.** Global magnetization ($m$), variance ($\chi$), Binder cumulant ($U_4$), and critical exponents ($
u, eta, \gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Alignment strength ($K$), particle density ($ho$), and the dimensionality ($d=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the transition can be characterized by standard scaling laws, though some notes mention limitations regarding symmetry breaking in the microscopic dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the order parameter vs. control parameter to show the transition, use Binder cumulants to locate critical points ($K_c$), and demonstrate finite-size scaling collapse using derived exponents.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the non-motile absorbing state, then introduces motility via a two-step process. The core analysis involves applying finite-size scaling to determine continuous transition characteristics, followed by detailed examination of density dependence and update scheme effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Activity, when it takes the form of motility, is generally seen to promote order in many-body systems. Here we present a one-dimensional lattice model that, in the non-motile limit, exhibits absorbing ferromagnetic states. When activity is introduced through biased motility, these absorbing state are destabilised and the system instead undergoes a transition from an ordered flock to a disordered state as the alignment strength is decreased. A finite-size scaling analysis of physical quantities reveals a continuous transition with critical exponents satisfying the hyperscaling relation in one dimension, providing quantitative evidence for the activity-induced disorder.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the minimum external work required to control two interacting Brownian particles with nonreciprocal coupling. It discovers a critical time $T_c$ beyond which the extractable work becomes infinitely negative, representing a thermodynamic anomaly. By imposing realistic physical constraints like finite force range, the authors show how this anomaly resolves into distinct, measurable phase-like transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of optimal control in non-equilibrium systems, especially identifying fundamental anomalies like unbounded work extraction, is highly relevant for understanding energy harvesting and dissipation in open quantum or classical many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper solves a finite-time thermodynamic optimal control problem for two nonreciprocally interacting Brownian particles confined by harmonic traps.

**Main result.** Nonreciprocity leads to a 'thermodynamic anomaly' where the extractable work is unbounded below for times exceeding a critical time $T_c$. Physical regularizations restore finite optima and reveal multiple transition types.

**Method.** The authors use optimal control theory, minimizing the mean external work functional by solving associated Euler-Lagrange equations for the system dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model consists of two overdamped Brownian particles coupled by nonreciprocal interactions within harmonic traps. The control manipulates the trap centers and separation distance over time $T$.

**Key observables.** Mean external work ($W$), output power, optimal protocol trajectories (oscillatory), and transition points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonreciprocity parameter ($\alpha$), critical time ($T_c$), trap range limits ($R_{	ext{max}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on solving the mean dynamics equations derived from the overdamped Langevin equation, and later introduces physical regularizations like finite force saturation or trap range.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optimal mean paths for both reciprocal and nonreciprocal cases, and show the minimum work landscape ($W^*$) versus duration $T$ highlighting the anomaly near $T_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the dynamics and the work functional. It then analyzes the non-reciprocal case to find the thermodynamic anomaly at $T > T_c$. Finally, it introduces physical regularizations (finite range/saturation) to restore a finite optimum, revealing distinct transition behaviors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We solve exactly a finite-time thermodynamic optimal control problem for two nonreciprocally interacting Brownian particles translated by two harmonic traps. The controller manipulates both the center and separation of the pair. Nonreciprocal interactions generate an internal active force that couples these two channels. The optimal protocol is oscillatory, deliberately opens the dimer even when the target separation is unchanged, and can extract work during transport. A central finding is a finite critical time beyond which the external-work infimum is $-\infty$: at any prescribed duration beyond this threshold, both extractable work and output power are unbounded. Physical regularizations such as finite trap range and force saturation restore a finite optimum and convert the anomaly into optimal-protocol transitions: in the zero-target case, a hard finite range produces a first-order-like jump from the zero protocol to a maximum-range protocol, whereas smooth force saturation gives a continuous, second-order-like onset. Under finite-range constraints, the optimal protocol can further undergo multiple finite-time transitions, producing multiple work-duration kinks with no qualitative analog in prior studies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the rugged energy landscape of LDPC codes, treating them as a model for spin glass physics. It uses advanced statistical mechanics sampling techniques to characterize metastable 'valleys' within this landscape. The key finding is the demonstration of ensemble inequivalence, showing that standard thermodynamic relations break down when analyzing these glassy states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on coding theory, the mathematical machinery developed—analyzing glassy dynamics, phase transitions, and ensemble equivalence in complex energy landscapes—is directly applicable to understanding many-body systems like spin glasses or frustrated quantum magnets.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates stable valleys within the complex, glassy energy landscape of a low-density parity-check (LDPC) code.

**Main result.** The work demonstrates that ensemble inequivalence can occur in these systems, specifically identifying regimes where valleys are microcanonically stable but canonically unstable.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced sampling techniques like the multicanonical method and adaptive Monte Carlo simulations to probe energy barriers and entropy landscapes.

**Model / system.** The system is a classical LDPC code defined on expander graphs, which models spin glass order without explicit quenched randomness. The Hamiltonian penalizes local configurations that violate the code constraints.

**Key observables.** Bulk free energy density ($f$), energy density ($\epsilon$), configurational entropy density ($s_{	ext{conf}}$), and the height of the entropy barrier ($\Delta S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$ or $T$), boundary condition parameters $(\alpha, \gamma)$, and minimum/maximum allowed values for energy ($E$) or magnetization ($M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the true entropy barrier by projecting the configuration space onto a single coordinate (like magnetization $M$), which is noted as a potential source of error.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between energy densities derived from different boundary conditions and show phase diagrams mapping out regions of canonical instability versus microcanonical stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using tree recursion, then applying advanced sampling methods (multicanonical) to characterize valleys, and finally comparing results across ensembles to pinpoint ensemble inequivalence boundaries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical low-density parity-check (cLDPC) codes defined on expander graphs are a fundamental ingredient in the construction of good quantum LDPC codes, a recent milestone in quantum error correction. They also define interesting statistical mechanics models in their own right, as they include examples of spin glass order without quenched randomness or frustration. We investigate this via a case study of a cLDPC code on a locally tree-like expander graph. Recursive techniques on trees, made possible by the locally tree-like property, probe a menagerie of stable, incongruent valleys induced by imposing different boundary conditions at low temperature. A complementary numerical study of the valleys on closed finite graphs reveals the inequivalence of the microcanonical and canonical ensemble for certain valleys.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous thermodynamic framework to define structural superlubricity by analyzing the limits of directional depinning stress at crystalline interfaces like graphene/hBN. The authors demonstrate that simple elastic reconstruction susceptibility ($\Lambda$) is insufficient to predict static pinning, concluding that under ideal conditions, the system remains in an elastically relaxed sliding regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state friction, the methodology of defining phase transitions via thermodynamic limits ($\lim_{A	o\infty}$) and distinguishing between different types of order parameters (e.g., susceptibility vs. true barrier height) is highly relevant to understanding phase boundaries in open quantum systems or complex many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to establish a rigorous thermodynamic criterion for structural superlubricity—the suppression of static friction—at two-dimensional crystalline interfaces.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that smooth, clean moiré continua cannot generate an extensive bulk sliding barrier; the results are consistent with an elastically relaxed sliding regime rather than a statically pinned phase.

**Method.** They define superlubricity using limits of directional depinning stresses ($	au_{	ext{dep}}$) and distinguish this thermodynamic criterion from reconstruction susceptibility ($\Lambda_n$).

**Model / system.** The study uses a two-dimensional discrete model, specifically examining the graphene/hBN interface, governed by an energy functional incorporating elastic terms and generalized stacking-fault energies.

**Key observables.** Directional depinning stress ($	au_{	ext{dep}}$), equilibrium restoring stress ($\Phi_R$), and reconstruction susceptibility ($\Lambda_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The thermodynamic limits $A	o\infty$ are crucial for defining the true phase; $\Lambda=1$ is identified as a reconstruction scale, not a static phase criterion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on taking the limit of large contact areas ($A	o\infty$) and assumes zero-temperature, defect-free, in-plane conditions for the primary conclusions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of reconstruction susceptibility ($\Lambda$) on twist angle and show finite-size flow of corrugation, demonstrating that small approximants do not predict large-scale behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the thermodynamic criteria using depinning stresses. It then analyzes continuum models to show that smooth reconstruction is insufficient for pinning. Finally, it applies these rigorous criteria to atomistic simulations (graphene/hBN) to test for resolved static barriers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Incommensurability and elastic reconstruction do not by themselves define a structurally superlubric phase. We define fully sliding and pinned zero-temperature phases by $\limsup_{A\to\infty}τ_{\rm dep}^{\max}(A)=0$ and $\liminf_{A\to\infty}τ_{\rm dep}^{\min}(A)>0$, respectively; $Λ_n=|V_n|G_{n,i}[D_{\rm rel}^{-1}(\mathbf q_n)]_{ij}G_{n,j}$ measures only reconstruction susceptibility. Translational covariance then proves that a clean, smooth, infinite moiré continuum can reconstruct without acquiring a bulk sliding barrier. We restore atomic sampling in a two-dimensional discrete model of graphene/hBN and test both a diffusion quantum Monte Carlo first-star potential and a 15-harmonic Leven potential across three rational approximants and five directions. No physical-coupling equilibrium or metastable barrier is resolved. The Leven spectrum raises the largest tested $Λ$ from $0.142$ to $0.212$, while artificial scaling through $Λ=1$ reaches uncontrolled strain before a size-independent threshold appears. The tested zero-temperature in-plane models are therefore consistent with an elastically relaxed sliding regime; $Λ=1$ is a reconstruction scale, not a static phase criterion.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an Arnold--Nielsen geometric deformation to model noncommutative dynamical optimal transport, embedding complexity directly into the underlying differential calculus. It establishes that this complex-weighted transport is equivalent to a simpler unweighted problem under certain conditions. The framework successfully applies these concepts to analyze both unitary and dissipative quantum evolution.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful generalization of optimal transport theory by incorporating intrinsic quantum complexity measures directly into the geometry of state space evolution. This has implications for understanding resource costs in open quantum systems beyond simple energy minimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a geometric framework by deforming noncommutative dynamical optimal transport using an Arnold--Nielsen complexity operator. The goal is to internalize the cost of quantum complexity directly into the differential calculus governing state evolution.

**Main result.** It proves that this complexity-weighted transport problem is equivalent to an unweighted problem generated by the deformed calculus, and identifies the distance on unitary orbits as a quotient metric from a right-invariant complexity geometry.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced functional analysis techniques involving noncommutative differential calculi, optimal transport theory (Wasserstein distance), and geometric methods applied to quantum state spaces.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum states described by density matrices ($ho_t$) evolving on von Neumann algebras. The dynamics are analyzed in the context of both unitary (Hamiltonian) evolution and dissipative (Lindblad) processes.

**Key observables.** Wasserstein distance ($W_\partial$), complexity operator ($G$), relative entropy ($	ext{Ent}_{\sigma}$), and specific preparation bounds for Bell/GHZ states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity weight $G$ is crucial, which can be defined based on the underlying dynamics (e.g., $\|L_k\|^2_{	ext{op}}$ in the Lindblad case).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include requiring the deformed quadratic form to remain Dirichlet for certain equivalences, and treating the Lindblad case as an entropy-gradient-flow background.

**Figures summary.** No specific figure summaries were extracted from the notes; the focus is on theoretical theorems and derivations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the complexity deformation of the calculus (Theorem A), establishing metric equivalences (Theorem B), deriving geometric structures on unitary orbits, and finally applying this framework to dissipative Lindblad dynamics via gradient flow analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We deform the Carlen--Maas--Wirth framework for noncommutative dynamical optimal transport by an Arnold--Nielsen type complexity operator. A positive state-independent operator $G$ compatible with the Hilbert bimodule structure of a noncommutative differential calculus $\partial\colon\M\to\Hcal$ can be absorbed into the calculus itself, \[ \partial_G:=G^{1/2}\partial. \] The corresponding complexity-weighted transport problem is exactly the unweighted transport problem generated by $\partial_G$, whenever the deformed quadratic form remains Dirichlet. In finite dimensions we prove existence of minimizers for density-dependent Petz-class metrics and for fixed physical complexity weights, the latter without commutation between $G$ and the state-dependent mobility. On unitary orbits we identify the induced distance with a quotient metric coming from a right-invariant complexity geometry. This yields an exact Bell-state preparation result via Clairaut's relation and an exactly computed restricted-path upper bound for GHZ preparation; the Lindblad detailed-balance case is included only as entropy-gradient-flow background.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models how viscoelastic fluids affect the beating motion of flagella using colloidal analogues. It finds that oscillatory (back-and-forth) motion can exploit fluid memory to significantly increase beating frequency, while continuous rotation behaves differently. The work provides a general mechanism for active enhancement in complex polymeric environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical soft matter dynamics, the concept of non-Markovian noise and energy dissipation mechanisms in active systems shares conceptual links with open quantum system dynamics where memory effects are crucial for decoherence or driving protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how viscoelastic effects, specifically fluid memory, influence the stochastic dynamics of active beating motions like those found in cilia or flagella.

**Main result.** Back-and-forth (rower) beating is identified as a generic mechanism that can exploit fluid memory to rapidly enhance beating frequency beyond a critical memory threshold. Conversely, unidirectional motion shows a different dependence on fluid memory.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical modeling based on generalized Langevin equations for colloidal models immersed in a Jeffreys fluid, solving the resulting non-Markovian dynamics using numerical integration schemes.

**Model / system.** Two minimal colloidal models are analyzed: a 'Rower' model simulating back-and-forth beating between harmonic potentials, and a 'Rotor' model simulating continuous unidirectional rotation. The surrounding medium is modeled as a Jeffreys fluid to capture viscoelastic memory effects.

**Key observables.** Mean beating period ($\langle T angle$), relative fluctuations in the beating period ($	ext{CV}^2$), and the steady-state frequency enhancement factor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fluid memory parameter ($	ilde{\lambda}$), polymeric drag coefficient ($eta$), and the critical memory threshold ($	ilde{\lambda}_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping non-Markovian equations to coupled Markovian forms using auxiliary variables, and comparing results in the small-noise limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the Rower and Rotor models, showing how $\langle T angle$ changes with fluid memory ($	ilde{\lambda}$) for both beating types, and plotting relative fluctuations ($	ext{CV}^2$) vs. $	ilde{\lambda}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem (viscoelastic influence on active motion), develops theoretical models (Rower/Rotor in Jeffreys fluid), presents numerical simulations varying memory parameters, analyzes key observables like frequency and fluctuations, and concludes by generalizing the findings to back-and-forth beating.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ciliary and flagellar beating often occurs in viscoelastic fluids. The surrounding fluid strongly influences the beating dynamics. Viscoelastic effects on beating dynamics, however, remain poorly understood. Here, we investigate the stochastic dynamics of experimentally realized colloidal models in a Jeffreys fluid. We find that back-and-forth beating transiently aligns the driving and polymeric forces, leading to a rapid increase in the beating frequency once the fluid memory becomes comparable to the stroke duration. The crossover is marked by a maximum in beating-period fluctuations. For unidirectional rotational motion, however, beating slows down with increasing fluid memory. Our results identify back-and-forth beating as a generic mechanism for exploiting fluid memory in active oscillators, providing a possible explanation for enhanced flagellar beating in polymeric fluids.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports discovering a Magnetoresistive Memory (MRM) effect in Eu5In2As6 that persists deep within the paramagnetic phase, unlike previously studied materials. The persistence of this history-dependent resistivity suggests an underlying mechanism rooted in short-range correlations or hidden order rather than long-range magnetic ordering. This establishes the material as a promising platform for quantum memory applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state magnetism, the investigation into history dependence, hidden order, and correlation between electronic transport and lattice structure touches upon non-equilibrium dynamics and emergent quantum states relevant to correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates a novel Magnetoresistive Memory (MRM) effect in Eu5In2As6, particularly focusing on its persistence and mechanism within the paramagnetic phase.

**Main result.** The MRM effect is observed well into the paramagnetic regime, suggesting underlying physics related to short-range correlations or hidden order rather than simple magnetic domain wall dynamics seen in other materials.

**Method.** Measurements include resistivity vs. applied field/temperature sweeps, time-dependent measurements, and X-ray diffraction analysis of lattice parameters.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the semiconductor compound Eu5In2As6, a magnetic material exhibiting A-type AFM ordering at 16 K. The study compares its MRM behavior to known systems like manganites and iridates.

**Key observables.** Resistivity ($ho$) hysteresis loops (untrained vs. trained states), lattice parameter contraction along the c-axis, and temperature dependence of the memory effect.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The MRM onset temperature (~30 K in some analyses) is significantly above the AFM transition temperature ($T_N = 16 	ext{ K}$); carrier concentration plays a critical role.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that resistivity relaxation and lattice contraction are intrinsic to the material's electronic state, ruling out simple Joule heating effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare MRM across different magnetic materials; others show $ho(T)$ curves for trained/untrained samples, field dependence of hysteresis loops, and lattice parameter changes correlated with the MRM onset temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically establishes the existence of MRM in Eu5In2As6 above $T_N$, contrasts it with known magnetic ordering mechanisms, investigates its coupling to structural changes (lattice contraction), and finally uses symmetry analysis to constrain potential underlying physical models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetoresistive materials that respond sensitively to applied fields are central to modern data storage technologies. Here we unveil a novel Magnetoresistive Memory (MRM) in Eu$_5$In$_2$As$_6$, where the electrical resistivity depends not only on the magnitude but also on the history of the applied magnetic field. Such an effect has been reported in only two classes of strongly correlated electron systems: perovskite manganites and pyrochlore iridates. In both cases, the effect has been observed in the magnetically ordered phase. It has been attributed to metastable magnetic states in manganites and conducting domain walls in iridates. Remarkably, the MRM in Eu$_5$In$_2$As$_6$ onsets at twice the antiferromagnetic transition temperature, well within the paramagnetic phase. The temperature, field, and time dependence of resistivity suggest that either a hidden order or a fluctuating phase with short-range correlations underlies this effect. Our results offer MRM as a new platform for quantum sensing and memory technologies, and encourage searching for MRM in related materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive spectroscopic and theoretical investigation into the graphene/CrSBr interface. Researchers demonstrate that strong interfacial coupling causes significant charge transfer and electronic reconstruction, leading to novel properties like an insulator-to-metal transition and evidence of magnetic proximity effects. These findings establish the system as a versatile platform for next-generation spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong focus on proximity effects, band engineering via external stimuli (strain, doping), and emergent magnetism in 2D van der Waals heterostructures is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron behavior in artificial quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To microscopically understand the electronic landscape governing emergent behaviors, such as unconventional quantum Hall effects, in the graphene/CrSBr heterostructure driven by proximity interactions.

**Main result.** The interface exhibits massive charge redistribution leading to hole doping of graphene and an insulator-to-metal transition in interfacial CrSBr, alongside evidence for magnetic proximity coupling via Dirac cone splitting.

**Method.** A combination of Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES), Low-Energy Electron Microscopy (LEEM), and Density Functional Theory (DFT) was employed to characterize the interface.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the graphene/CrSBr van der Waals heterostructure, investigating how interfacial charge transfer, strain, and magnetic coupling modify the electronic structure of both components.

**Key observables.** Interfacial charge redistribution, spin-polarized conduction band population, insulator-to-metal transition, splitting feature in the Dirac cone, and compressive strain effects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The study investigates phenomena under various doping/strain conditions, focusing on electronic structure renormalization distinct from conventional doping.

**Assumptions / limitations.** DFT calculations are used to provide qualitative insight into band alignment and strain trends, while experimental measurements map the actual electronic reconstruction at the interface.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $	ext{CrSBr}$ structure, ARPES spectra along high symmetry paths, DFT-calculated bulk/interface electronic structures, and Fermi surface maps showing charge transfer effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting exotic responses in the heterostructure. It then presents spectroscopic characterization (ARPES/LEEM) to map the interfacial electronic state, supported by DFT calculations that model charge transfer, strain, and magnetic coupling mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stacking van der Waals materials provides a powerful route to engineer emergent electronic and magnetic behaviors through proximity-driven interactions. The graphene/CrSBr heterostructure has emerged as a compelling platform in this frontier, exhibiting exotic macroscopic responses-- including uniaxial surface-plasmon-polariton propagation and an unconventional quantum Hall effect-- indicative of strong interfacial electronic and magnetic coupling. However, a microscopic electronic landscape governing these phenomena has remained elusive. Here, we provide a comprehensive spectroscopic characterization of the graphene/CrSBr interface using a combination of angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES), low-energy electron microscopy (LEEM), and density functional theory. We resolve a massive redistribution of interfacial charge that concurrently hole dopes graphene and populates the quasi-one-dimensional spin-polarized conduction band of CrSBr, resulting insulator-to-metal transition in the interfacial CrSBr layer. Furthermore, electronic structure of CrSBr exhibits strong momentum-dependent renormalization distinct from conventional charge doping, and theoretical modeling supported by Raman spectroscopy points to additional interfacial compressive strain. In addition, ARPES reveals a splitting-like feature in the graphene Dirac cone consistent with spin degeneracy lifting, providing possible evidence of magnetic proximity coupling. These findings provide crucial microscopic insight into the system's optical and transport responses and establish graphene/CrSBr as a versatile platform for charge-transfer control, strain-driven band engineering, magnetic-proximity coupling, and directionally confined excitations for next-generation spintronic and nanophotonic devices.

</details>
