**Summary.** This theoretical study explores toroidal transitions, a subtle multipole moment, in atoms under strong magnetic fields. It derives the relevant Hamiltonian by coupling an atomic spin operator to the displacement current density. The authors conclude that while these transitions are theoretically possible, their observation is severely challenged by dominant E1 backgrounds and requires searching in low-lying states using advanced differential measurement techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work pushes the boundaries of observable atomic physics by predicting and theoretically analyzing transitions beyond standard electric/magnetic multipoles. The detailed treatment of magnetic field effects (diamagnetic coupling) provides crucial context for interpreting future high-field spectroscopic measurements in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the theoretical possibility of observing toroidal transitions, a lesser-known multipole moment, in hydrogenic and alkali atoms.

**Main result.** Toroidal transitions are expected to be extremely weak compared to dominant electric dipole (E1) transitions, necessitating advanced differential measurement techniques for detection.

**Method.** The authors derive the spin-toroidal Hamiltonian by coupling the atomic spin-toroidal operator ($\mathcal{C}_s$) to Maxwell's displacement current density ($\mathbf{J}_d$).

**Model / system.** The system involves hydrogenic and alkali atoms subjected to a large static magnetic field ($B_0$) and illuminated by an optical driving field. The analysis uses the Dirac equation framework, incorporating diamagnetic coupling effects.

**Key observables.** Toroidal Dipole ($	ext{T}1$) transition strength relative to $	ext{E}1$ transitions; visibility quantified by state mixing (trace distance).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low principal quantum numbers ($n < 4$) are suggested as favorable search regions due to diamagnetic effects; high magnetic fields (e.g., $B \sim 886$ T) are required for optimal decoupling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The $	ext{E}1$ contribution is assumed to always dominate, requiring differential measurement; the analysis relies on approximations valid for $n 
eq n'$ and the use of the Rydberg relation for resonance conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate parity properties of multipoles ($	ext{E}, 	ext{M}, 	ext{T}$), compare $	ext{E}1$ vs. $	ext{T}1$ transitions under circular polarization, and show how diamagnetic mixing limits spin-orbit decoupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the toroidal Hamiltonian from fundamental coupling terms ($\mathcal{C}_s$ and $\mathbf{J}_d$), analyzes the resulting matrix elements using recurrence relations, discusses the impact of diamagnetism on state mixing, and proposes differential measurement strategies to isolate the weak signal.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In addition to electric and magnetic multipoles, the expansion of current density also yields toroidal terms, a lesser-known family of multipoles. A recent proposal, I. Kuprov $\textit{et al.}$, Science Adv. 8 abq6751 (2022), explores the possibility of a direct observation of optical toroidal transitions in hydrogen and alkali atoms in the presence of a large magnetic field that decouples the spin and the angular momentum of the electron. However, the difficulty of observing these transitions against the nearby electric dipole (E1) transitions were underestimated because of extra admixture coming from diamagnetic coupling. Here, we revisit the toroidal coupling in atoms, taking diamagnetic contribution into account, and discuss the technical challenges of observing toroidal coupling in atomic physics. We show that toroidal transition should be searched in transitions with low principal quantum numbers. The remaining strong electric-dipole contribution could be removed using an appropriate differential measurement.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces McQuack, a novel and efficient quantum kernel method designed for multiclass classification. It overcomes major bottlenecks in existing quantum kernel approaches by using a class-centroid fidelity matrix, achieving linear scaling with data size. The results demonstrate strong performance both in simulation and on real quantum hardware, while also confirming the model's trainability.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the practical limitations of applying quantum computation to machine learning tasks by proposing a scalable, trainable kernel method suitable for near-term noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to develop an efficient, trainable quantum kernel method for multiclass classification that overcomes the quadratic scaling and fixed nature of traditional full-Gram kernels.

**Main result.** The proposed McQuack kernel achieves linear scaling in both training samples and classes, outperforming pure quantum baselines in simulation and matching RBF performance on hardware inference without explicit training.

**Method.** McQuack replaces the full Gram matrix with a trainable sample-to-(class-centroid) fidelity matrix, allowing for shared parameters across all class classifiers.

**Model / system.** The method is evaluated using quantum kernels defined by state fidelity $|\langle\psi_j|\psi_iangle|^2$, implemented on 124-qubit subgraphs of IBM quantum devices and simulated environments.

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy (compared to RBF kernel, SVC, MLP), training complexity ($O(n_{	ext{train}} M)$), and the absence of barren plateaus up to 13 qubits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Training parameters ($\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{b}$) and class centroids ($\mathbf{c}_m$); hardware qubit count (up to 124).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that classes can be well-represented by centroids in the Hilbert space; training on actual quantum hardware is currently infeasible due to long runtimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 12 illustrates the circuit architecture used to implement a quantum kernel measurement (probability of measuring the all-zero state).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces McQuack, details its linear scaling mechanism using class centroids, provides theoretical background on QKMs and Kernel Target Alignment, evaluates performance in simulation against baselines, tests hardware inference, and analyzes trainability diagnostics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Kernel methods are powerful tools in machine learning but commonly used full-Gram kernels face three key limitations: (1) quadratic scaling with training set size; (2) the use of fixed, non-trainable kernels; and (3) the absence of an intrinsic formulation for multiclass classification. We present McQuack, a trainable quantum kernel method for multiclass problems that achieves linear scaling in the number of training samples. This is accomplished by replacing the full training-set Gram matrix with a trainable sample-to-(class-centroid) fidelity matrix. We evaluate the model in simulation and on 124 qubits of two IBM devices, across more than 150 datasets. In simulation, McQuack outperforms existing "pure" quantum baselines, while results from hardware inference -- obtained without training -- achieve performance similar to an RBF kernel. Finally, we study the trainability of the model and observe no evidence of barren plateaus in our experiments with up to 13 qubits, and highlight the importance of parameter initialization for successful optimization.

</details>

### [Angular Momentum Quantization of a Charge-flux Composite: Quantum Electrodynamic Approach](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20283v1)

**Authors:** Kicheon Kang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20283v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.20283_figures/2607.20283_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. A two-dimensional quantum analog of Feynman’s disk paradox. Two inﬁnitesimally close, counter-rotating loops carrying opposite charges generate the central magnetic ﬂux. A weak internal dissipative interaction, such as radiative damping, slows the loops to a complete rest. This allows the transfer of their mechanical angular momentum to the sur- rounding vacuum via the emission of photons (γ). Meanwhile, the adiabatic switching oﬀof the ﬂux induces an azimuthal electric ﬁeld that imparts an additional angular momentum of exactly +qΦ/2π to the external charge q via Faraday’s law of induction.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper resolves the paradox of angular momentum quantization for charge-flux composites by moving beyond semiclassical models to a full Quantum Electrodynamic treatment. The key finding is that the interaction with the vacuum electromagnetic field generates a compensating angular momentum term. This ensures the total angular momentum strictly adheres to integer or half-integer quantization, confirming the robustness of fundamental symmetries in 2D quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical understanding of how gauge-invariant interactions (like those mediated by vacuum fields) can enforce fundamental symmetries and conservation laws on exotic quantum systems, which is relevant for understanding topological order or fractional statistics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper resolves a conceptual conflict regarding the quantization of angular momentum for a two-dimensional charge-flux composite when treated as an isolated system in free space.

**Main result.** By employing a full Quantum Electrodynamic (QED) approach, the authors show that the interaction between the charge and flux generates an intrinsic angular momentum that exactly compensates for the fractional part of the kinetic angular momentum, restoring strict integer or half-integer quantization.

**Method.** The analysis uses Noether's theorem applied to a full QED Lagrangian formalism, contrasting these results with insufficient semiclassical Hamiltonian treatments.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional charge-flux composite (anyon). The theoretical framework involves coupling the particle dynamics to the vacuum electromagnetic field via a QED Lagrangian.

**Key observables.** Total angular momentum ($\mathbf{L}$), kinetic angular momentum ($\langle L_k angle$), and interaction angular momentum ($\langle L_{	ext{int}} angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The flux $\Phi$ and the charge $q$ are key parameters determining the fractional part of the initial expectation value.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The semiclassical approach is insufficient; a full QED treatment incorporating vacuum field interactions is necessary to resolve the quantization paradox.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the two-dimensional quantum analog of Feynman’s disk paradox, detailing flux generation and adiabatic switching off.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts semiclassical results (which fail) with a rigorous QED derivation using Noether's theorem. It identifies the compensating interaction term that restores quantization, while also noting the failure of standard field definitions in 2D due to boundary terms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The fractional angular momentum of a two-dimensional charge-flux composite is a well-established phenomenon usually derived from a semiclassical Hamiltonian. However, when the composite is treated as an isolated system in free two-dimensional space, the fundamental rotational and reflection symmetries of the $O(2)$ group demand that its total angular momentum is strictly quantized. We address this conceptual discrepancy by applying a full quantum electrodynamic (QED) approach combined with Noether's theorem. We demonstrate that the interaction between the charge and flux, mediated by the vacuum electromagnetic field, generates an intrinsic interaction angular momentum composed of both field momentum and hidden relativistic momentum. This gauge-invariant interaction angular momentum exactly compensates for the fractional part of the kinetic angular momentum. Consequently, the net angular momentum of the composite strictly follows the integer or half-integer quantization rule. Our formalism clarifies that the conventional fractional spin corresponds to the expectation value of the kinetic angular momentum within the perturbed QED ground state. It also elucidates why the standard classical field angular momentum definition fails to capture this in two dimensions, due to non-vanishing boundary terms.

</details>

### [Anticoncentration of the Permanent in Ginibre Ensembles](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20329v1)

**Authors:** Frederic Koehler, Pui Kuen Leung  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20329v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">

<img src="2607.20329_figures/2607.20329_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1. Monte Carlo comparison of the normalized determinantal benchmark ∆K 3 and normalized squared permanent XK 3 for 3×3 Ginibre matrices over K ∈{R, C, H}. Over H, the benchmark uses the Study determinant and the permanent is row-ordered. The estimates use 3×106 samples in each field; shaded bands show two standard errors. One Gaussian row is integrated out analytically.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves the 'Anticoncentration' property for the permanent of Ginibre random matrices over real, complex, and quaternionic fields. By comparing the permanent to the determinant using advanced tools like Laplace transforms, it resolves a major conjecture (PACC) and establishes tight bounds on the distribution of this key random matrix observable.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly mathematical, random matrix theory is foundational to quantum physics, especially in characterizing spectral statistics and entanglement measures derived from Gaussian ensembles. The study of permanents versus determinants touches upon non-Gaussian correlations relevant in complex physical models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the 'Anticoncentration of the Permanent' for Ginibre ensembles, a problem related to the permanent Anticoncentration Conjecture (PACC). It seeks to establish bounds on the distribution of the normalized row-ordered permanent.

**Main result.** For $K=\mathbb{C}$, the authors resolve the PACC by proving that the normalized permanent has a radial density with specific concentration bounds. They also established general anticoncentration results for $\mathbb{R}, \mathbb{C},$ and $\mathbb{H}$ fields.

**Method.** The proof employs advanced techniques comparing the squared Gaussian permanent to the squared determinant in Laplace-transform order, utilizing inductive arguments and characteristic functions derived from properties of Gaussian distributions over different algebras ($\mathbb{R}, \mathbb{C}, \mathbb{H}$).

**Model / system.** The model involves $n 	imes n$ random matrices ($G_n^{\mathbb{K}}$) whose entries are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) standard $\mathbb{K}$-Gaussian variables, where $\mathbb{K}$ can be the real ($\mathbb{R}$), complex ($\mathbb{C}$), or quaternionic ($\mathbb{H}$) numbers.

**Key observables.** The normalized row-ordered permanent $W_n^{\mathbb{K}} = 	ext{per}_{\mathbb{K}}G_n^{\mathbb{K}}/\sqrt{n!}$, and its associated radial density $p_n^{\mathbb{K}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $eta = \dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K} \in \{1, 2, 4\}$, and the matrix size $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on comparing moments and distributions using Laplace transforms ($\le_{Lt}$), which is shown to be a strong tool but requires careful handling across different algebraic structures.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but notes mention Monte Carlo comparisons showing sign changes in CDF differences for $\Delta_K^3 - X_K^3$ versus non-negative signs for Laplace transform differences.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops results by first establishing benchmarks using the determinant and Study determinant across different fields. It then uses inductive arguments, characteristic functions, and specialized comparison theorems (like Lemma C.5) to prove the anticoncentration bounds for the permanent itself.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Let $\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}\}$, put $β=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}$, and let $G_n^{\mathbb{K}}$ be an $n\times n$ matrix with i.i.d. standard $\mathbb{K}$-Gaussian entries, namely a standard $\mathbb{K}$-Ginibre matrix. We prove that the normalized row-ordered permanent $W_n^{\mathbb{K}}=\operatorname{per}_{\mathbb{K}}G_n^{\mathbb{K}}/\sqrt{n!}$ has a radial density $p_n^{\mathbb{K}}$ satisfying $\|p_n^{\mathbb{K}}\|_\infty=p_n^{\mathbb{K}}(0)\lesssim_βn^{(β+2)/4}$ and $\sup_{z\in\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{P}(|W_n^{\mathbb{K}}-z|\leq\varepsilon)\lesssim_βn^{(β+2)/4}\varepsilon^β$. In particular, for $\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}$, this resolves the Permanent Anticoncentration Conjecture of Aaronson and Arkhipov. The proof compares the squared Gaussian permanent with the squared (Study) determinant in Laplace-transform order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the measurement bottleneck in Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) by developing 'efficiently simulable' variants. These methods allow the necessary expectation values to be computed efficiently using free-fermionic or IQP circuits, rather than requiring costly quantum measurements. This work establishes a strong classical baseline for evaluating quantum approaches to hard combinatorial optimization problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of classically simulable quantum encoding schemes provides crucial theoretical tools for benchmarking the scaling and power of future quantum hardware implementations in optimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is the measurement overhead associated with Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) when estimating expectation values for combinatorial optimization problems.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'efficiently simulable PCE' variants, which allow all necessary expectation values to be computed classically, providing a powerful dequantized baseline for future quantum algorithm development.

**Method.** They construct specific classes of Pauli correlation encodings using free-fermionic evolutions (via matchgate circuits) or IQP circuits, ensuring classical tractability of the required measurements.

**Model / system.** The framework encodes classical binary variables into expectation values of many-body Pauli observables. The simulation is instantiated using free-fermionic Hamiltonians and general IQP ansatzes parameterized by rotations.

**Key observables.** Signs of Pauli expectation values ($	ext{sgn}(\langle \Pi_i angle_	heta)$), the normalized merit factor ($ho_F$), and solution quality on benchmarks like MaxCut and MDKP.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required precision $\epsilon$ dictates measurement cost, which is a key focus for improvement; problem sizes range from tens to thousands of variables.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the necessary expectation values can be generated by choosing a 'compatible pair' $(\Pi, U)$ such that classical simulation is efficient, bypassing costly quantum measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare FF-PCE and IQP-PCE performance on MaxCut and MDKP instances, showing competitive solution quality. Figure 8 specifically compares the approximate LABS merit factor ($ho_F$) for different PCE variants against local search baselines.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of standard PCE due to measurement overhead. It then introduces and details the construction of efficiently simulable PCE using free-fermionic and IQP circuits. Finally, it benchmarks these methods on several combinatorial optimization problems (MaxCut, MDKP, LABS) to validate their performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) is a heuristic framework for binary optimisation that encodes classical variables into many-body Pauli observables. While PCE requires fewer qubits than other approaches, it relies on estimating a large number of Pauli expectation values whose signs determine the variables' values, which can incur substantial measurement overhead. Here, we introduce efficiently simulable PCE, a class of dequantised PCE realisations where all expectation values needed can be computed efficiently classically. We instantiate this idea using free-fermionic evolutions, realised by matchgate circuits, and Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial (IQP) circuits. On MaxCut, Maximum Independent Set, Multi-Dimensional Knapsack, and Max3SAT benchmarks, these methods produce high-quality solutions across problem sizes ranging from tens to thousands of variables. Our results show that PCE is naturally understood as a correlation-based optimisation framework with both quantum and classically simulable realisations. This yields a dequantised baseline for evaluating future quantum PCE implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes an exact theoretical benchmark for the static exchange-only local field factor in the uniform electron gas. By deriving this universal function, the authors critically test and demonstrate the quantitative failures of several established approximations (like BDL or McLVP) near the Fermi surface singularity at $2k_F$. The findings illuminate that observed physical behavior is governed by a precise cancellation between correlation and exchange effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electron gas theory, the rigorous benchmarking of correlation effects at finite momentum ($q 
eq 0$) provides fundamental insights into how screening modifies particle interactions, which is crucial for understanding correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish an exact, universal benchmark for the static local field factors of the uniform electron gas (UEG), particularly addressing unexplained features near $2k_F$.

**Main result.** The paper derives and presents the exact exchange-only local field factor, $G_x(q,0)$, which shows specific limiting behaviors ($q^2/4$ at small $q$, $\pi^2/6$ at $2k_F$, and $1/3$ at large $q$).

**Method.** The authors combine textbook local-field relations with exact static exchange polarizabilities derived from QMC calculations to construct a closed-form, universal benchmark.

**Model / system.** The system is the Uniform Electron Gas (UEG), analyzed in the static limit ($\omega=0$) using density functional theory concepts applied to correlation functions.

**Key observables.** Static local field factors ($G^+$), the exchange-only factor $G_x(q,0)$, and spin-resolved components of these factors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The wavevector $q$, normalized by $2k_F$ (using variable $Q$), and the density parameter $r_s$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that QMC representations are smooth enough to reveal underlying systematic cancellations between correlation and exchange contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the exact universal $G_x(q,0)$ against approximations from older theoretical kernels (BDL, McLVP), showing significant quantitative discrepancies in the comparison plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem by noting unexplained features in QMC data; it then derives the exact benchmark formula for $G_x$; subsequently, it rigorously compares this benchmark against established approximations and analyzes the underlying cancellation mechanisms near $2k_F$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The static local field factors of the uniform electron gas are known accurately from quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) calculations, but two of their most striking features have lacked a mechanism: $G_+$ grows almost exactly as $q^2$ up to nearly $2k_F$, and the violent $2k_F$ physics of the Fermi surface leaves only a faint trace in it. Combining the textbook first-order local-field relation with the exact static exchange polarizability gives a compact exact benchmark: the universal exchange-only local field factor $G_x(q,0) = -I(Q)Q^2/L(Q)^2$, $Q = q/2k_F$, with exact values $(1/4)(q/k_F)^2$ at small $q$, $π^2/6$ at $2k_F$ -- apparently never before stated as a local-field value -- and $1/3$ at large $q$, giving the exchange-only spin-averaged contact value $g(0) = 1/2$ of the unpolarized gas (BDL 1977). $G_x$ rises to a finite maximum of 1.9924 at $1.9685k_F$ and passes through $π^2/6$ at exactly $2k_F$ with a slope diverging as $\ln^3|q-2k_F|$. Against this benchmark, the 1980 BDL tabulation is accurate to about one percent below $1.5k_F$, while the 2024 McLachlan-variational kernel of Nazarov and Silkin is two-percent accurate only below $0.5k_F$, is a factor of 1.74 low at $2k_F$, and its large-q limit is inconsistent with the contact constraint. A spin-resolved decomposition of QMC local field factors about the exact baseline shows that, within the resolution and smoothness assumptions of the QMC-based representations, both features follow from a strong exchange-correlation cancellation: at the QMC point closest to $2k_F$ ($q = 2.01k_F$, $r_s = 2$), correlation cancels 0.43(7) of the exact exchange value 1.33. Computed consistently at each wavevector, the static exchange-kernel thresholds derived from the closed form reproduce, to 0.2%, the finite-wavevector charge threshold $r_s \approx 10.6$ near $q = 1.94k_F$ obtained numerically in 1980, and recovered again in 2014.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a geometric framework using Lie group theory to analyze the redundancy inherent in Variational Quantum Circuits. By mapping the search space onto a homogeneous manifold defined by the stabilizer group of the Hamiltonian, it provides an explicit formula for the effective degrees of freedom. This theoretical insight is validated through numerical experiments on VQE optimization.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools (homogeneous space geometry) to quantify how much information is truly encoded in variational quantum circuits, which is crucial for designing efficient algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the redundancy and effectiveness of Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) by characterizing the geometry of the space of achievable effective Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The dimension of this effective Hamiltonian manifold is determined by the structure of a stabilizer group $S$, yielding $\dim(	ext{MH}) = \dim(U(V)) - \dim(S)$.

**Method.** The authors use Lie group theory, identifying the space as a homogeneous quotient space ($S\setminus U(V)$), and employ spectral decompositions to derive tractable dimension formulas.

**Model / system.** The model involves VQAs (like VQE) acting on an $n$-qubit system defined by a Hamiltonian $H$. The circuit search space is mapped onto the orbit space $	ext{MH}$ under unitary transformations.

**Key observables.** Effective Hamiltonians ($	ext{MH}$), Stabilizer Group ($S$), and the Overlap Score $s(A)$ used to diagnose generator redundancy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the system ($n$) and the structure/eigenspaces of the Hamiltonian $H$ (determining $\dim(S)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on treating the problem using the mathematical framework of Lie groups and homogeneous spaces.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometric setup showing how unitary evolution maps observables, identifying the redundant component $S$. Figure 2 shows the distribution of overlap scores $s(A)$ for tested circuit generators.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical connection between observable redundancy and quotient spaces using Lie theory. It then derives dimension formulas based on the stabilizer group structure. Finally, it applies these concepts to a numerical VQE experiment, testing generator candidates using the overlap score $s(A)$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study redundancy and effectiveness of Variational Quantum Circuits via algebraic and geometric views of Lie groups. Considering unitary transformations acting on Hermitian observables, a stabilizer group decomposition is given. Subsequently, we identify the Hermitian orbit with a quotient space of a symmetric space. Through this connection, we characterize the effective circuit degrees of freedom. Our approach of spectral decompositions and homogeneous spaces yields tractable calculation criteria, which are verified by numerical experiments.

</details>

### [On the global well-posedness for the nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation with the weighted Sobolev initial data on the line](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19649v1)

**Authors:** Zhenya Yan, Guoqiang Zhang, Guancheng Zhu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19649v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper proves the global well-posedness for the nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation using an advanced Inverse Scattering Transform approach. The methodology involves solving associated Riemann-Hilbert problems and overcoming technical hurdles related to non-standard symmetries in the spectral problem. The result confirms that unique solutions exist globally in time, which is crucial for modeling physical systems like optical pulse propagation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on nonlinear PDEs rather than quantum dynamics directly, the use of advanced spectral methods (IST/RH problems) to prove long-time existence is a powerful mathematical tool applicable to analyzing complex quantum field theories or open quantum systems governed by integrable Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing the global well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for the reverse space-time nonlocal Fokas-Lenells (nFL) equation on the line.

**Main result.** Global existence and uniqueness of solutions are proven, and the associated solution map is shown to be Lipschitz continuous on the admissible initial-data class.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on developing an Inverse Scattering Transform (IST) framework formulated via associated Riemann-Hilbert (RH) problems and utilizing spectral uniformization transforms.

**Model / system.** The model is the nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation, $q_{xt} - q + iqq^{PT} = 0$, which generalizes integrable models like NLS and is relevant to modeling ultrashort optical pulses in nonlinear media.

**Key observables.** The potential $q(x, t)$, reflection coefficients ($r_j$), and the associated scattering data are key observables used throughout the analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weighted Sobolev initial data regularity: $q_0(x) \in H^3(\mathbb{R}) \cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})$, and a quantitative smallness condition on the initial data is required for technical steps.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof requires establishing uniform bounds on reflection coefficients by assuming a smallness condition on the initial potential, which ensures positive definiteness of the Hermitian part of the jump matrix.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 outlines the general scheme: direct scattering $ightarrow$ time propagation $ightarrow$ inverse scattering. Figure 2 details analytical regions in the spectral plane ($\lambda$-plane and $z=\lambda^2$-plane).

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard IST approach: defining the direct scattering problem, constructing associated Riemann-Hilbert problems, developing the necessary transforms (like spectral uniformization), proving global reconstruction via controlled singular integral operators, and finally establishing the Lipschitz continuity of the solution map.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish the global well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for the reverse space-time nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation with the weighted Sobolev initial data $q_0(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}) \cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})$ on the line. We develop the inverse scattering transform formulated via the associated Riemann-Hilbert problems to study this issue. A spectral uniformization transform is introduced to resolve the singular behavior inherent in the KN-type negative flow spectral problem. Owing to the reverse space-time reduction, reflection coefficients no longer satisfy the usual Hermitian conjugation symmetry, and the coercivity of the jump matrix is therefore not available a priori. The quantitative smallness condition on the initial data yields uniform bounds on the reflection coefficients and ensures the uniform positive definiteness of the Hermitian part of the associated jump matrix. The resulting coercivity allows us to establish the bounded invertibility of the associated singular integral operator through a Fredholm and vanishing-lemma argument. Under this condition, we prove an $L^{2}$-Sobolev bijective correspondence between the potential and scattering data, exclude spectral singularities on continuous spectra, and obtain the global existence and uniqueness of solutions. Moreover, the associated solution map is Lipschitz continuous on the admissible initial-data class.

</details>

**Authors:** Seongmin Kim, Abhinav Rijal, Yuri Alexeev, Nora Bauer, Martin Roetteler, Mina Yoon, George Siopsis, In-Saeng Suh

**Summary.** This paper introduces DQAOA-GPT, a hybrid framework that accelerates solving hard combinatorial problems by replacing iterative quantum circuit optimization with direct circuit generation using a GPT model. By leveraging this AI acceleration during problem decomposition, the method drastically reduces computational cost while maintaining high solution quality for large-scale instances.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on optimization rather than fundamental AMO physics, the reliance on quantum circuit generation via a generative AI model represents a novel computational paradigm that could accelerate simulations or parameter estimation in complex open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Solving large-scale, NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems that are intractable using standard variational quantum algorithms.

**Main result.** The proposed DQAOA-GPT significantly reduces the computational cost of solving sub-problems compared to conventional DQAOA while maintaining competitive solution quality, especially for larger problem sizes.

**Method.** It integrates Distributed QAOA (DQAOA) with a GPT model that is trained to directly generate high-quality quantum circuits for decomposed sub-problems in a single inference pass.

**Model / system.** The framework uses DQAOA to decompose the large optimization problem into smaller, manageable sub-problems. These sub-problems are then encoded onto graphs and solved using parameterized quantum circuits generated by the GPT model.

**Key observables.** Solution quality (accuracy) and computational runtime/cost reduction relative to conventional DQAOA.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sub-problem size ($n$), number of variables ($N=100$ benchmark), and the depth/structure of the generated quantum circuits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that decomposing the problem into smaller, locally optimized sub-problems is sufficient for finding a near-optimal global solution. The GPT model replaces iterative optimization with direct generation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 shows that DQAOA-GPT maintains relatively constant runtime while achieving improved relative accuracy as the sub-problem size ($n$) increases, contrasting sharply with standard DQAOA's rapidly increasing cost.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the difficulty of combinatorial optimization. It then details the DQAOA decomposition strategy and introduces the GPT training pipeline (tokenization, transformer architecture). Finally, it presents the inference loop where the generated circuits are used within the DQAOA framework for benchmarking.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While combinatorial optimization problems are central to many scientific and engineering applications, their solution remains challenging due to exponentially large search spaces. Variational quantum algorithms offer a promising route for tackling such problems, yet their practical performance is limited by repeated quantum circuit evaluations and classical parameter updates. In this work, we introduce DQAOA-GPT, a hybrid framework that integrates the distributed quantum approximate optimization algorithm (DQAOA), which decomposes a large optimization problem into smaller sub-problems, with GPT-based quantum circuit generation for solving those sub-problems. Rather than relying on iterative variational optimization, the proposed approach uses a trained generative model to directly generate high-quality quantum circuits for the decomposed sub-problems. As a benchmark, we evaluate DQAOA-GPT against conventional DQAOA on dense HUBO optimization problems with up to 100 decision variables. The results demonstrate that DQAOA-GPT significantly reduces computational cost while maintaining competitive solution quality, with larger acceleration observed for larger sub-problem sizes. Although this work focuses on benchmark-scale validation, the framework provides a promising foundation for larger-scale combinatorial optimization in hybrid HPC-QC environments through increased GPU resources and parallel computing capability.

</details>

### [Hybrid LLM-Guided Search for Quantum Reservoir Architecture Design](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19506v1)

**Authors:** Krishna Bhatia, Gautami Sanjay Naik  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19506v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<img src="2607.19506_figures/2607.19506_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">

**Summary.** This paper treats Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) architecture design as an optimization problem and tests if Large Language Models can guide this search. By comparing various strategies, the authors show that combining LLM proposals with classical evolutionary operators yields superior performance on temporal forecasting tasks. The key takeaway is that LLMs are powerful high-level controllers when integrated into robust, hybrid optimization frameworks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It explores the intersection of advanced AI (LLMs) with quantum information processing hardware simulation, providing a methodology for automating the design space exploration crucial for near-term quantum machine learning applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to optimize the complex design choices inherent in Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) by framing it as a constrained black-box architecture search problem.

**Main result.** A hybrid approach combining Large Language Model (LLM) proposals with classical optimization operators significantly outperforms random search, demonstrating that LLMs are useful high-level controllers rather than universal optimizers for QRC design.

**Method.** The authors developed a simulator-based benchmark comparing several search policies—including Random Search, Evolutionary Search, TPE, and an LLM-Hybrid agent—to optimize QRC parameters across three temporal forecasting tasks.

**Model / system.** The core system is Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC), which utilizes fixed quantum dynamics to generate a high-dimensional feature map for subsequent classical readout. The search space involves numerous architectural choices like qubit count, reservoir depth, and entanglement topology.

**Key observables.** Task-level predictive performance scores (error rates) on three benchmarks: NARMA10, Mackey-Glass forecasting, and temporal parity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit range (3–8), Reservoir Depth (1–12), various encoding/topology parameters, and a fixed evaluation budget of 25 evaluations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study is confined to simulator-based benchmarks using small reservoirs and does not claim quantum advantage or hardware performance; the improvement relies on embedding LLMs within validated hybrid search loops.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the closed-loop QRC architecture-search workflow, comparing mean/standard deviation results across tasks (Table II), and showing relative error improvements over random search (Fig. 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by defining QRC's design complexity, presents the simulator benchmark framework, compares five distinct search policies using three temporal tasks, analyzes performance metrics, and concludes with architectural insights regarding LLM utility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum reservoir computing (QRC) uses fixed quantum dynamics as a high-dimensional temporal feature map and trains only a lightweight classical readout. QRC is attractive for near-term quantum machine learning, but its performance depends strongly on architecture choices such as input encoding, reservoir depth, entanglement topology, measurement features, state-reset policy, feature construction, and readout regularization. We introduce \method, a simulator-based benchmark that formulates QRC design as constrained black-box architecture search and evaluates whether large language models can act as proposal controllers for this search problem. The benchmark compares five policies under identical evaluation budgets: random search, evolutionary search, Bayesian/TPE optimization, a feedback-based LLM agent, and \hybrid, which combines LLM proposals with memory, mutation, crossover, duplicate avoidance, and exploration. On NARMA10, Mackey-Glass forecasting, and temporal parity, \hybrid{} is the most consistent policy: it ranks first on NARMA10 and temporal parity and second on Mackey-Glass, narrowly behind evolutionary search. Under a 25-evaluation budget and three seeds, \hybrid{} improves over random search on all tasks, including a 23.6\% relative reduction in Mackey-Glass error. The results do not show that LLMs are universal QRC optimizers; rather, they show that generative models can be useful high-level controllers when embedded inside validated, reproducible hybrid search loops.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a cluster-based extension to Reinforcement Learning (RL) for optimizing Belief Propagation (BP) decoding of QLDPC codes. By allowing the RL agent to select and update entire clusters of nodes in parallel, it significantly reduces the serial scheduling depth compared to node-by-node updates. This achieves an attractive balance between high error correction performance and low computational latency.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses efficiency bottlenecks in quantum error correction decoding by introducing hardware-aware parallelism into an ML-driven scheduling framework, which is highly relevant for implementing fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The performance of Belief-Propagation (BP) decoding for Quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (QLDPC) codes is limited by short cycles and degeneracy, and existing learned scheduling methods suffer from poor within-iteration parallelism.

**Main result.** The proposed cluster-based RL approach preserves most of the error-rate benefit of fine-grained sequential scheduling while substantially reducing the number of required scheduling decisions per BP iteration, offering a superior latency-parallelism tradeoff.

**Method.** The authors extend Reinforcement Learning (RL) by partitioning Variable Nodes (VNs) into clusters. The RL agent selects an entire cluster to update in parallel, using a quantized, permutation-invariant histogram state representation for the Markov Decision Process.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on decoding QLDPC codes used in fault-tolerant quantum computation. Decoding involves message passing over a Tanner graph structure defined by parity-check matrices.

**Key observables.** Error rate (Block Error Rate/BER) vs. physical error probability ($p_x$), and the number of scheduling decisions required per BP iteration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cluster size ($B$), quantization resolution ($L$), and the total iteration budget ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an ideal parallel cluster-update model for performance gains, and that a quantized state representation is sufficient to capture necessary scheduling information.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare BER across various decoders (QBP, unclustered RL-S, clustered RL-S) showing that the clustered approach maintains high performance while drastically reducing the required number of scheduling decisions per iteration compared to the unclustered baseline.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of standard BP and existing sequential RL methods. It then proposes the cluster-based extension, detailing the state representation (histogram/quantization) and formulating the problem as a cluster-level MDP. Finally, it presents numerical results demonstrating the improved latency-parallelism tradeoff.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Belief-propagation (BP) decoding for quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) codes is attractive due to its low complexity, but its performance is often limited by short cycles, degeneracy, and convergence failures. Recently, reinforcement-learning-based sequential variable-node (VN) scheduling (RL-S) was shown to improve BP decoding by learning state-dependent update orders. However, the VN-by-VN nature of that approach offers limited within-iteration parallelism, since only one VN is updated at a time. In this paper, we propose a cluster-based extension of RL-S for QLDPC codes. The VNs are partitioned into fixed clusters, and at each scheduling step the RL agent selects one cluster to update, after which all VNs in that cluster are updated in parallel using the same pre-update incoming messages. To keep the tabular state space practical for large cluster sizes, we introduce a permutation-invariant cluster state based on a normalized histogram of local mismatch weights, followed by quantization. This representation makes the number of cluster states depend on the quantization resolution rather than the cluster size. We also develop the corresponding cluster-level Markov decision process, reward function, and Q-learning update. Numerical results on representative QLDPC codes show that the proposed clustered learned scheduling preserves most of the error-rate benefit of VN-level learned sequential scheduling while substantially reducing the number of scheduling decisions per BP iteration, thereby providing an attractive latency-parallelism tradeoff.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents detailed analytical solutions to the Schrödinger equation using the Killingbeck potential, which combines Coulombic, linear, and harmonic terms. The authors employ sophisticated series expansion methods and compare results with Biconfluent Heun Equation formalisms to determine energy eigenvalues. Crucially, they also correct mathematical misstatements found in previous academic treatments of similar quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous analytical solutions for a complex confining potential relevant to heavy quark systems, offering advanced mathematical techniques applicable to non-standard quantum potentials beyond simple Coulomb or harmonic forms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work focuses on deriving analytical solutions for the Schrödinger equation using a specific hybrid potential (Killingbeck potential) and correcting mathematical errors in related literature.

**Main result.** The authors derive explicit expressions for bound-state solutions and eigenfunctions, yielding energy eigenvalues that match expected limits when analyzed through multiple formalisms.

**Method.** The primary method involves the series expansion technique applied to the radial Schrödinger equation, supplemented by comparison with Biconfluent Heun Equation (BHE) formulations.

**Model / system.** The system is governed by the Killingbeck potential, $V(r) = -A/r + Br + Cr^2$, which models physical scenarios like quarkonium spectroscopy and hydrogen-like atoms in plasma environments.

**Key observables.** Energy eigenvalues ($\epsilon_{n,l}$), bound-state solutions, and eigenfunctions for the system.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potential parameters (A, B, C) and quantum numbers ($L$, $n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes a specific polynomial form for the solution function $F(r)$ and relies on setting coefficients of powers of $r$ to zero.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical problem and the master equation, then details the series expansion method leading to recurrence relations. It presents multiple derivations for energy eigenvalues (series truncation vs. BHE) before concluding with corrections to prior literature.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The work presents analytical solutions to the Schrodinger equation for the Killingbeck potential, a Hybrid model combining harmonic, linear and Coulomb terms, and which is also an approximate model of Yukawa-type potentials. The radial Schrodinger equation is solved by means of the series expansion method, thus yielding the exact expressions of both bound-states solutions and eigen-functions for the systems; these systems include quarkonium and confined hydrogen-like atoms in plasma environments. Furthermore, we offer a constructive commentary on the work of Obu et al. (East Eur. J. Phys. 3, 146-157, 2023), with the aim of clarifying a mathematical misstatement used in their analytical treatment of analogous systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Qoreo, a novel choreographic programming language designed to simplify and formalize the development of complex quantum distributed protocols. By viewing the entire system as one global choreography rather than many independent processes, it ensures type safety and guarantees deadlock freedom. This provides a powerful, high-level abstraction layer for designing reliable quantum network applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on high-level programming semantics rather than physical Hamiltonian dynamics, the rigorous type-theoretic guarantees for distributed operations are crucial for designing reliable quantum network protocols that might interact with AMO platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Programming distributed quantum systems requires coordinating complex sequences of operations, making direct process modeling tedious and error-prone.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Qoreo, a choreographic programming language that guarantees type safety and projects to deadlock-free process networks for well-typed protocols.

**Method.** They propose a choreographic paradigm where the entire protocol is viewed as a single global program, which is then formally projected onto independent processes using Endpoint Projection (EPP).

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum distributed systems utilizing qubits and incorporating local quantum computation with inter-actor classical and quantum communication.

**Key observables.** Deadlock freedom, well-defined quantum operations, and the successful execution of protocols like Quantum Key Distribution (B92).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity scales exponentially with the number of qubits; linear types enforce physical constraints like no-cloning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is that choreographies lack room for actor autonomy, complicating security analysis and dynamic participation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum teleportation protocols both as informal circuits and as global choreographies, contrasting this with the process model view.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the state-of-the-art in distributed quantum computing to formalizing Qoreo's local quantum language (with linear types), developing the full choreographic language, defining EPP, and finally discussing implementation via extraction to NetQASM.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programming distributed quantum systems requires multiple actors to coordinate precise sequences of quantum operations, classical communication, and entanglement generation. Writing such protocols directly as distributed processes is tedious and error-prone, and subtle mismatches can cause deadlock or silently incorrect quantum states. We present Qoreo, a choreographic programming language for quantum distributed systems in which an entire protocol is expressed as single, global program (a choreography) rather than as a collection of independent actor processes. Qoreo includes a local quantum language with linear types that enforce the no-cloning principle; a choreographic language that combines local quantum computation with inter-actor classical and quantum communication; and a process language for individual network nodes. We prove type safety for choreographies, guaranteeing that well-typed programs implement well-defined quantum operations, and we define endpoint projection~(EPP), which automatically derives a network of independent processes from any choreography. We prove EPP sound and complete with respect to the choreographic semantics; as a corollary, every well-typed choreography projects to a deadlock-free process network. The metatheory of Qoreo is fully mechanized in Rocq, and we provide an extraction pipeline to NetQASM for simulation and deployment on quantum network hardware.

</details>

**Authors:** Michael A. Perlin, Matthew Steinberg, Ben Criger

**Summary.** This paper introduces an adaptive simulated annealing method to efficiently search for high-performing quantum error-correcting codes, focusing on CSS and SWEL types. By optimizing a surrogate energy function related to logical error rates, the authors discover state-of-the-art codes whose parameters approach theoretical bounds. These findings provide promising candidates for building fault-tolerant quantum computation circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental resource limitations in quantum computation by providing concrete constructions of highly efficient error-correcting codes suitable for near-term hardware demonstrations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is searching for quantum error-correcting codes that possess both high encoding rates and large distances.

**Main result.** The authors found state-of-the-art CSS and SWEL codes whose distances meet or exceed the quantum Gilbert-Varshamov bound for small block lengths (up to 50 qubits).

**Method.** An adaptive simulated annealing algorithm is employed, guided by an energy function that approximates the logical error rate in a code-capacity noise model.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum stabilizer codes, specifically CSS codes and a subclass called SWEL codes. These are defined over physical qubits and characterized by their parity check matrices ($H$).

**Key observables.** Code distance ($d$), encoding rate ($k/n$), and the ability of logical gates to be implemented transversally.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Block lengths up to 50 physical qubits; code parameters $(n, k, d)$; quantum Gilbert-Varshamov bound variants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The search relies on simulated annealing as a heuristic optimization technique due to the exponentially large space of possible codes. The search for automorphisms in related work is noted as non-exhaustive.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, reviews theoretical bounds (Hamming, QGV), details the adaptive simulated annealing optimization strategy using an energy function surrogate, and presents results on finding high-performing CSS and SWEL codes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce an adaptive simulated annealing algorithm to search for moderately-sized quantum error-correcting codes with high encoding rates and large distances. Our search targets two classes of stabilizer codes: (1) CSS codes, and (2) a subclass of CSS codes that we call ``self-dual with equivalent logicals'' (SWEL) codes, the latter of which which admit transversal implementations of logical Hadamard and phase gates that can be leveraged to construct fault-tolerant gate sets. The search is guided by an energy function that acts as a surrogate for the logical error rate in a code-capacity noise model, combining code distance with a count of minimum-weight logical operators to resolve the discrete plateaux that impede naïve distance optimization. For block lengths of up to $50$ physical qubits, our search finds state-of-the-art CSS and SWEL codes whose distances frequently meet or exceed the variants of the quantum Gilbert-Varshamov bound. In addition to providing favorable seed codes for fault-tolerant architectures based on code concatenation, the codes found in this work are promising candidates for high-rate code demonstrations on near-term quantum computing hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a simulation recipe using qubits to study how minimum length scales, derived from the Generalized Uncertainty Principle (GUP), affect quantum systems. By analyzing low- and high-energy limits on a discretized grid, the authors reveal unique dependencies on the lattice spacing ($\Delta L$). The work offers a computational tool to test theoretical predictions about quantum gravity effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The systematic incorporation of finite lattice spacing into fundamental quantum relations like the uncertainty principle provides a concrete, computational method to explore regimes inaccessible by standard continuum theory in quantum gravity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a framework to simulate quantum gravity phenomena by analyzing the Generalized Uncertainty Principle (GUP) in both low- and high-energy regimes.

**Main result.** It establishes that incorporating finite grid spacing ($\Delta L$) leads to distinct GUP behaviors, suggesting novel pathways to investigate minimum length effects at high energies, though conflicts arise when enforcing consistency with special relativity versus preserving the standard HUP.

**Method.** The authors use a first-quantization approach on a discretized spatial grid ($L$ qubits) to derive discrete position and momentum operators, allowing for systematic calculation of expectation values under GUP corrections.

**Model / system.** The model analyzes one-dimensional quantum systems using localized ansatz states (Gaussian/sinc) simulated via qubit representations. The core physics revolves around modifying the standard uncertainty relation due to hypothesized minimum length scales from quantum gravity.

**Key observables.** Position uncertainty ($\delta x$), momentum uncertainty ($\delta p_x$ and $\delta p_x^H$), and the resulting GUP inequality $(\delta x)(\delta p) \geq \dots$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Minimum grid spacing ($\Delta L$), low/high energy regimes, mass ($m$), and fundamental constants ($\hbar$, $c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a finite $\Delta L 
eq 0$ for simulation purposes; approximations are made using Taylor expansions when deriving the high-energy momentum operator ($\hat{p}_{H x}$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares low-energy GUP curves from simulation data (Gaussian/sinc states) against theoretical lines. Figure 2 shows mass-dependent high-energy GUP properties for an electron using varying qubit numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the low-energy GUP effects due to discretization ($\Delta L$), then developing a procedure for high-energy momentum operators ($\hat{p}_{H x}$), and finally analyzing the resulting high-energy GUP, highlighting conflicts between relativistic consistency and HUP preservation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a recipe for simulating one-dimensional quantum systems for low and high energies with $L$ qubits within the framework of first quantisation. Assuming a minimum grid spacing $ΔL$ in the finite-difference method, the generalised uncertainty principle (GUP) is derived analytically and shows distinct properties for low- and high-energy quantum systems. In the low-energy regime, the GUP approaches to the standard Heisenberg uncertainty principle (HUP) for $ ΔL \ll \hbar$. However, for finite $ΔL \neq 0$, the GUP mathematically provides three different regions dependent on the average momentum and suggests that a wavefunction can exhibit a single-point localisation at the finite momentum uncertainty due to lack of grid resolution. We then derive a new expression for the high-energy momentum and focus on two specific cases relevant to relativistic aspects. First, if the HUP is relaxed and the high-energy momentum is matched to the special-relativistic one, the resulting GUP predicts the existence of a minimum length with non-zero mass for high energy despite the continuous limit $ΔL = 0$. Second, enforcing consistency with the HUP allows the recovery of a canonical high-energy representation with no minimum length. But this requirement brings incompatibility with the special-relativistic momentum and suggests a modified energy-momentum equation. Therefore, we believe that the proposed momentum formulation provides a novel pathway to investigate quantum gravity phenomena for high energy using quantum simulation tools.

</details>

### [Quantum Kernels and the Cross-Section of Stock Returns: Anatomy of a Vanishing Advantage](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20168v1)

**Authors:** Junchi Shen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20168v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 2: Short-sample study, 60 windows. Top: regularized geometric difference g between quantum kernels and the tuned RBF control. Bottom: within-window validation rank ICs of the three kernels.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously tests if quantum kernels can predict stock returns better than classical methods using financial time series. The authors find that apparent quantum advantages are artifacts of flawed testing protocols, such as using hindsight data or unequal training budgets. They propose strict standards for future empirical claims of quantum advantage in complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to finance, the methodology—developing strict protocol standards for claiming 'quantum advantage' in empirical data—is highly relevant to any field where theoretical quantum predictions meet noisy, real-world measurements (e.g., characterizing subtle effects in condensed matter experiments).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether quantum kernels provide a genuine, exploitable advantage over established classical machine learning methods for predicting cross-sectional stock returns.

**Main result.** Under rigorous, point-in-time evaluation protocols with sufficient statistical power, no statistically significant quantum advantage was found; penalized linear models consistently outperformed or matched the quantum kernel performance.

**Method.** The authors employed a 'kernel-swap control' protocol, comparing Quantum Kernel Ridge Regression (QKRR) against classical controls like RBF kernels and linear regressions using walk-forward out-of-sample testing on financial data.

**Model / system.** The system is the Chinese A-share stock market return cross-section. The models involve quantum encoding via an IQP-style layer to generate a fidelity kernel, which is then compared against classical kernels and linear predictors.

**Key observables.** Information Coefficient (IC), $\Delta$IC difference, $p$-values, Sharpe ratio, and the performance of various regression models over 170 walk-forward windows.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The evaluation is highly sensitive to protocols: point-in-time universes, budget equalization, and multiplicity correction are critical parameters for determining significance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption tested is that the market structure allows for a stable, exploitable quantum advantage; the paper concludes this assumption is likely false due to methodological artifacts in prior literature.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare rolling IC differentials between quantum and classical kernels, showing how apparent advantages vanish when accounting for sample size or budget constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by testing QKRR against controls. It then details rigorous evaluation protocols (kernel-swap, point-in-time) across many windows, systematically debunking spurious quantum advantages through statistical corrections and diagnostic analyses of kernel geometry vs. out-of-sample gains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Do quantum kernels improve cross-sectional stock return prediction? We run a controlled horse race on the Chinese A-share market in which a quantum fidelity kernel, a projected quantum kernel, and a classical RBF control share identical training subsamples, solver, and tuning budgets, so that only the kernel is exchanged. On the main evaluation -- a point-in-time universe and 170 walk-forward windows (2012-2025) -- no quantum advantage exists: the fidelity kernel is indistinguishable from its RBF control ($Δ$IC $=+0.005$, $p=0.42$), and a $2\times2$ design crossing kernel type with training budget (a Nystrom extension to the full ~38,000-observation windows) shows quantum kernels matching, but never beating, equal-budget linear models; after family-wise correction no pairwise difference among eleven models is significant, with point estimates favoring penalized linear regressions throughout. We then document how the opposite conclusion arises: a 60-window evaluation on a universe screened with full-sample information makes the same quantum kernel appear dominant on stability criteria and significantly better than neural baselines. Interaction characteristics from the anomalies literature help nothing, quantum or classical; a widened bandwidth grid reveals an interior optimum rather than the near-classical endpoint a coarse grid suggests; and the geometric difference, while large throughout ($g \gg 1$), does not predict out-of-sample gains ($ρ=-0.20$). We propose protocol standards -- kernel-swap controls, budget-equalized comparisons, point-in-time universes, and multiplicity-robust inference -- for empirical claims of quantum advantage in finance.

</details>

### [Schrödinger's real-valued equation revisited](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20104v1)

**Authors:** Oliver Passon, Bernd Rosenow  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.20104v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: The local real phase plane (𝑢, 𝜋∕ℏ) at a fixed point. (a) Free evolution rotates every energy mode clockwise at its frequency 𝜔𝑘= 𝐸𝑘∕ℏ, drawn for 𝐸𝑘&gt; 0 (Sec. 5.3); the Born density is the squared radius 𝑢2 + 𝜋2∕ℏ2, the conserved 𝐴2 𝑘of each mode. (b) Spinless time reversal 𝔗0 ∶(𝑢, 𝜋) ↦(𝑢, −𝜋) is the reflection in the 𝑢-axis – the real form of complex conjugation (Sec. 10). (c) A gauge transformation rotates the pair by the position-dependent angle 𝜒(𝐫), Eq. (145), while the vector potential shifts as 𝐴𝑖↦𝐴𝑖+ (ℏ∕𝑞) 𝜕𝑖𝜒, Eq. (149).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously examines attempts to reformulate quantum mechanics using only real variables instead of the standard complex wave function. It demonstrates that while the explicit imaginary unit can be absorbed or hidden, the underlying mathematical structure—the symplectic form—must persist. The necessity of this structure is highlighted by analyzing failures in single-field reductions when magnetic fields are introduced.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it probes the fundamental mathematical structure underpinning quantum theory, touching upon gauge symmetries and the geometric interpretation of complex numbers in physical observables.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether quantum mechanics, typically formulated with a complex wave function ($\psi$), can be equivalently described using purely real-valued variables.

**Main result.** While the explicit imaginary unit ($i$) can be removed or hidden in specific formulations (like Majorana fields), the underlying symplectic and complex structure it encodes must reappear, often manifesting as non-local relations or canonical partners.

**Method.** The authors employ mathematical reduction techniques by splitting $\psi = u+iv$ into real components, comparing this to phase-space reconstructions, and analyzing specialized formulations like those involving Majorana fields.

**Model / system.** The study applies to general quantum mechanical systems governed by the Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}$), examining both non-relativistic (Schrödinger) and relativistic (Dirac/Majorana) contexts, including interactions with magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Probability density ($ho = u^2 + v^2$), probability current, angular momentum expectation values ($\langle L_z angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength ($B$), coupling constants ($q, e$), and the structure of the Hamiltonian operator $\hat{H}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption tested is that a complete local description can be achieved solely with real variables; this fails when magnetic fields are present or requires non-local reconstruction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Zeeman effect splitting in angular momentum states, showing how the degeneracy is resolved into two counter-rotating branches ($\Omega^+, \Omega^-$) due to the magnetic field coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from reviewing historical real-valued attempts (single-field reduction), identifying failures under magnetic fields (non-locality via $\hat{H}^{-1}$), and concluding by comparing these findings with advanced structures like Majorana fermions in relativistic quantum mechanics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Schrödinger equation can be rewritten as a second-order equation for a single real-valued scalar field. Taken at face value, however, this one-field reduction obscures several local structures of quantum mechanics: the Born density and current, the role of momentum as a generator, and the usual derivation of the uncertainty relation. We argue that these difficulties do not show that real variables fail. They show that the reduced equation is not, by itself, a complete local representation of the theory. Its coherent real form is obtained by restoring the underlying Hamiltonian phase-space structure, where the missing component reappears as a canonical partner. In this real phase-space formulation, the standard structures reappear locally, and minimal magnetic coupling reveals an internal \(SO(2)\) gauge structure. Thus, the symbol \(i\) can be removed, but the symplectic and complex structure it encodes cannot.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using THz fields to control spin transport in an asymmetric quantum ring structure, mimicking the function of a spin transistor without physical gates. By leveraging Floquet theory and geometric phase effects, it achieves high-contrast switching and precise spin manipulation. This offers a robust, ultrafast alternative for spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from topological physics (Aharonov-Casher phase), periodically driven systems (Floquet engineering), and mesoscopic transport, offering a novel platform for ultrafast spintronics that avoids static material limitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to create a high-speed, contactless electromagnetic analog of the Datta–Das spin field-effect transistor using time-varying THz fields.

**Main result.** The interplay between topological Aharonov-Casher phase and dynamic Floquet modulation enables both ultra-high-contrast current suppression (an 'optical shutter') and precise spin control in a 1D quantum ring.

**Method.** The analysis employs the scattering matrix formalism adapted to the high-frequency Floquet–Magnus expansion, treating the system as an open quantum system coupled to reservoirs.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a 1D quantum ring with an asymmetric ($\pi/2$) configuration of quantum point contacts. The dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian subjected to a linearly polarized THz electric field.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization $P(I)$, current suppression contrast, and the evolution of the relative spin phase difference $\Delta\varphi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** THz frequency ($\omega$), Fermi energy ($E_F$), structural asymmetry ratio ($\pi/2$ configuration), and laser intensity ($I$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the high-frequency limit, utilizing the length gauge transformation to simplify the time dependence via the Floquet-Magnus expansion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches THz field interaction paths in the ring arms; later sections discuss intensity-driven transport characteristics near exceptional points (EPs).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using the $S$-matrix and Floquet theory to analyzing the effective Hamiltonian, and finally detailing how asymmetry and external driving fields induce spin control via interference effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Within the scattering matrix ($S$-matrix) framework adapted to the high-frequency Floquet--Magnus formalism based on the length gauge, we investigate spin-dependent quantum transport in a 1D quantum ring with an asymmetric ($π/2$) configuration of quantum point contacts. We demonstrate the realization of a contactless electromagnetic analog of the Datta--Das spin field-effect transistor with ferromagnetic leads operating at zero static magnetic field. It is shown that an off-resonance terahertz (THz) field enables high-precision switching between spin channels without altering the static parameters of the nanostructure. Driven by an electronic Vernier effect, the quantum interference in the asymmetric geometry yields a selective spin phase rotator alongside an ultra-high-contrast current-suppression regime (``optical shutter''). The proposed architecture is fundamentally robust against multiphoton leakage sidebands, offering a thermally immune, high-speed alternative to conventional semiconductor static spin transistors.

</details>
